‘Summer School, 2001,’ the First Czech-Vietnamese Feature, Is Coming to America via Cinemas, Amazon (Exclusive)

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Two diaspora groups can hit Cinemark theaters from Dec. 11 for a limited run of the award-winning debut feature directed by Dužan Duong, courtesy of Reel Citrus, before two big studio tentpole releases hit.

In 2025, Summer School, 2001, the debut feature of writer-director Dužan Duong and dubbed the “first Czech-Vietnamese feature film,” made a splash at the Karlovy Vary International Film Festival and beyond. Now, it is coming to America, courtesy of Reel Citrus, an L.A.-based distributor of Asian and diaspora films.

The company will open Summer School, 2001 in a limited U.S. theatrical release on Dec. 11 at Cinemark cinemas, hitting screens before Dune 3 and Avengers: Doomsday opens a week later. The film will then stream on Amazon from Dec. 18 before rolling out globally. Related Stories Movies Claes Bang, Valerie Pachner Drama 'I Is Another' Sells Wide Ahead of AFM Business The Hollywood Reporter Launches Turkish Edition

“At a time when immigration and belonging are front-page news in both the U.S. and Europe, the release brings two diasporas into the same theater,” said Reel Citrus.

Summer School, 2001 follows 17-year-old Kien, who returns to his family’s market stall in Cheb, a small town on the Czech border, after spending 10 years in Vietnam. It becomes a chaotic summer, during which the family must try to become whole again.

After its Karlovy Vary world premiere, the movie drew more than 20,000 moviegoers during its run in Czech cinemas. At the Czech Lion Awards, the Czech national film honors, Summer School, 2001 won the best screenplay award for Duong, Jan Smutný and Lukáš Kokeš, along with the best supporting actor honor for Dũng Nguyễn, the first Vietnamese actor to win a Czech Lion. The Czech Film Critics’ Awards named it the best screenplay and the Discovery of the Year.

At Viet Film Fest, the largest U.S. festival for the Vietnamese diaspora, the film won the Bronze Drum (Trống Đồng) Award for the best feature film, the best actor honor for Tô Tiến Tài and the Audience Choice Award for best feature.

Reel Citrus says it chose the theaters to target the communities that the film, which is in Vietnamese and Czech with English subtitles, speaks to. Most of the cinemas are in areas with large Vietnamese American populations, including Southern California, where it plays in Huntington Beach next to Little Saigon, the Bay Area, Houston, Dallas, Seattle, Las Vegas and the Washington, D.C. area. The rest of the theaters are in Chicago, Cleveland, Pittsburgh and Central Texas, “home to strong Czech, Polish and other Central and Eastern European communities,” the company explains.

Vietnamese American families and Czech and Polish moviegoers came out in droves for a screening of Summer School, 2001 at the Avalon Theatre in Washington, D.C. last March. Co-presented with the Embassy of the Czech Republic, it served as an early test for Reel Citrus, which says it releases films “in person, one community at a time.”

Reel Citrus acquired North American rights across all media, along with worldwide streaming rights, to Summer School, 2001 from AZN kru.

“Summer School, 2001 was an eye-opening film for us,” said Brittany Tran, founder & managing director of Reel Citrus. “Many of us grew up knowing the Vietnamese American story, but we knew far less about the Vietnamese families who built their lives in the Czech Republic and across Europe. Our diaspora is global, and when Vietnamese communities in the U.S. see the communities in Europe, we realize how much we share.”

She added: “We know we’re opening the week before Dune and Avengers, so we’re competing with the gods. But audiences who rarely see their own stories on the big screen are exactly who this release is for.”

And Duong, who talked to THR about his creative vision for Summer School, 2001 and his other creative work, shared: “Vietnamese families have been part of Czech life for decades, but our stories rarely reach the screen. I made this film for my community in Cheb and for every family that has had to find its way back to each other. Seeing it open in American theaters, in front of Vietnamese and Czech audiences together, means more than I can say.”

Check out a trailer for Summer School, 2001 here.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Testamentary Trust - Making sure money goes to kids, not a new spouse

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I cannot help you but maybe my anecdote can provide some insight as a cautionary tail.

My father, 3 children including myself, and stepmother, 2 children, were married 30+ years. It was a good marriage and I'm glad he found her.

The agreement, as stated by them dozens if not hundreds of times over the years, was that any inheritance would be split between the two families equally. 50% to her children, 50% to his children. A few years ago they paid for a grantor trust, primarily to avoid probate in California, and retitled their wealth into the trust with the exception of a few outlying annuities and such that just hadn't been cleaned up yet. The current wealth level is about 3.5 to 4 million.

She is now in the early stages of dementia and he passed from cancer about 6 months ago. We didn't discover until after his death how much he had been covering for her. As the annuities needed to be handled, and new paperwork filled out, it's become clear that she no longer understands the process or what it's supposed to do. When filling out paperwork, like car titles or beneficiaries on annuities, she is unwilling to put the trust. She wants to know why she can't leave her kids something, she says it makes her feel good, and we try to explain that the trust puts everything in a big pot and they'll get half when she passes but she doesn't absorb it. The effect of this is that she's slowly but surely been removing things from the trust and putting her own children as the direct beneficiaries. In the next few years she'll end up selling their real estate to go into care of one sort or another and move it into other accounts outside the trust. The major cash accounts are now being 'handled' by her daughters husband who is sure he can 'grow it'. It's likely that there will be little, if anything, actually distributed to his kids.

I want to be really clear that this is NOT her fault. There is no one to blame here. She has become unable to process higher level accounting and administration but not yet at a point where we can reasonably stop her or get her to accept input. We cannot protect her against herself at this stage, much less ourselves.

While I am well off, my two siblings and one her children as well could really have used the financial help while he was still alive. By delaying until after his death, he's guaranteed they will receive no help at all. I urge you to give with a warm hand, which is the only way to guarantee that the assistance goes to where you want it to go. If you cannot bring yourself to do that, then I also urge you to protect the inheritance against yourself. As we get older, there is no reason to believe that we'll be able to understand what we have put in place 20 years before. Good luck.
You have a more generous spirit than I do.

It seems that there is room here for your stepmother’s children to step in and stop their mother from effectively disinheriting you & your siblings. Especially given that they must know their mother is losing her cognitive abilities.

This situation may not be her fault, but her kids aren’t blameless.

I’m sorry you are going through this.

Packers’ perfect Jonathan Taylor trade offer to Colts before NFL trade deadline

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Packers' perfect Jonathan Taylor trade offer to Colts before NFL trade deadline appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

A 45-27 win over the New England Patriots on Sunday helped boost the Green Bay Packers to 6-3. While the Packers remain in last place in the NFC North, they have more than a fighting chance at a playoff spot. Josh Jacobs made his second start at running back of the season, following injuries and off-field issues, so it looked as if Green Bay had put its backfield issues behind it.

However, Jacobs had a terrible day in the backfield, running the ball four times for just five yards. Jacobs also failed to add any catches. Unfortunately, the money that the Packers have invested in the former first-rounder might continue to look like a sunk cost at this point.

Luckily for the Packers, there could be an answer to their rushing woes on the trade market. After firing GM Chris Ballard, the Indianapolis Colts have begun a teardown. A few days ago, the Colts dealt veteran leaders Quentin Nelson and DeForest Buckner to two separate NFC West contenders, the Seattle Seahawks and the San Francisco 49ers. Indianapolis still has a few veteran pieces they could move ahead of the trade deadline.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Most notably, that includes star running back Jonathan Taylor. In nine games, Taylor has 161 carries for 656 yards and eight touchdowns. While the standout back has yet to break the 100-yard barrier in a game this season, he's run for over 90 yards in five games. However, with the Colts getting pummeled by the Jacksonville Jaguars last weekend, the odds of Indianapolis making a postseason run at 2-7 seem far-fetched. So, what would it take for Packers GM Brian Gutekunst to get Taylor to Lambeau Field?

Although Taylor has had a couple of down games over the last two weeks, the 27-year-old is still one of the NFL's best backs. In Indianapolis, he's currently being wasted. The offensive line just lost Nelson, so Taylor lost his best blocker. The play calling from head coach Shane Steichen hasn't helped matters either.

With the trade deadline less than 24 hours away, it feels like the Colts have failed to get an offer that they'd like. Enter the Packers. Although the offense had a superb day in the win over New England Sunday, the rushing game still needs a jump start. Former Pittsburgh Steelers third round pick Kaleb Johnson, acquired before the season, led the team with 68 yards and a touchdown on 14 carries. Jacobs also lost carries to Pierre Strong Jr., who wasn't in the picture when the season started.

Gutekunst needs to call the Colts front office and figure out if a 2028 second round pick would get the job done. If not, combine that offer with a fourth or fifth rounder from 2027 and see if that gets the job done. It might take a little more, but if Gutekunst wants to give the Packers their best shot at a Super Bowl run, then adding more firepower to head coach Matt LaFleur's attack wouldn't be a bad idea. If the Green Bay can bring Taylor north, their title contention claims will certainly have more weight behind them.

Sierra Club Welcomes CalPERS’ $71 Billion Climate-Solutions Investment Milestone, Urges Fund to End Financing of Fossil Fuel Expansion

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

SACRAMENTO, CA — On Monday, the California Public Employees’ Retirement System (CalPERS) reported that the value of investments it classifies as climate solutions reached $71 billion as of June 30, 2026, up from $59.7 billion a year earlier. The increase brings the nation’s largest public pension fund closer to its goal of $100 billion in climate investments by 2030.

Sierra Club welcomed CalPERS’ continued commitment to climate-solutions investing and called on the fund to adopt stronger standards and transparency around what counts toward its $100 billion goal. Sierra Club also urged CalPERS to prioritize investments that support real-world emissions reductions and address climate risks across its broader portfolio to protect the retirement security of the workers and retirees it serves.

In response, Ben Cushing, Director of Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“It’s encouraging to see CalPERS make progress toward its climate-solutions goal, but a growing investment total doesn’t tell us how much new money is helping build clean energy and cut emissions. Reducing climate risk also means looking at where CalPERS invests across the whole portfolio. CalPERS has already taken the important step of using its votes to hold company directors accountable for how they handle climate risk. It can build on that work by adopting a policy to stop financing fossil fuel expansion and backing climate projects that need new investment to get built or expand.”

CalPERS said the $11.3 billion increase came from both new investments and gains on assets it already held. CalPERS also reported that financed emissions across the asset classes covered by its analysis rose by approximately 6 percent over the past year, largely reflecting investments in higher-emitting assets that it expects to decarbonize over time. For its global public equity and U.S. corporate credit holdings, CalPERS reported an implied temperature rise of 2.9°C, up from 2.7°C a year earlier.

Last year, Sierra Club and the California Common Good coalition urged CalPERS to publish a full list of the investments it counts toward its $100 billion goal, adopt science-based standards for what qualifies as a climate solution, and exclude fossil fuel investments from that total. Sierra Club has raised these concerns at CalPERS board meetings, and elaborated on them in a December 2025 CalMatters op-ed questioning the credibility and real-world impact of CalPERS’ reported climate investments.

Sierra Club’s 2025 Principles for Climate Solutions Investments outline standards for credible investing, including prioritizing real-world emissions reductions and establishing safeguards for transition financing. Its January 2026 Climate Solutions Gap report likewise highlighted CalPERS as an example of why ambitious climate-solutions targets must be backed by credible definitions, clear eligibility criteria, and transparent reporting.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

21X and LayerZero Report 10,000 Onchain Order Matches Per Second in Pilot

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: European trading venue 21X and LayerZero say a private, permissioned deployment of Zero handled 10,000 order matches per second in a month-long technical pilot. The companies disclosed the result on November 4, reporting a 45-fold performance improvement against the EVM layer-two baseline they used.

European trading venue 21X and LayerZero say a private, permissioned deployment of Zero handled 10,000 order matches per second in a month-long technical pilot. The companies disclosed the result on November 4, reporting a 45-fold performance improvement against the EVM layer-two baseline they used. It is an unusually specific benchmark in a market full of throughput promises, although an internal pilot is not the same as sustained production trading under real market stress.

The partners say they ran a fully onchain central limit order book with an emphasis on US equity tokens. Matching orders onchain is technically different from recording a trade after an offchain matching engine has already decided the outcome. The reported throughput would therefore address a genuine challenge for blockchain-based venues. The private instance also matters: network membership and visibility can be controlled differently from a permissionless public chain. Comparing the result with public networks requires care because validators, transaction patterns, hardware assumptions and network conditions may not be equivalent.

Regulated markets need surveillance, market-access controls, records, resilience and procedures for trade errors. Some institutions are unwilling or unable to expose all transactional details on a public ledger. A permissioned environment can allow more predictable operations while still using programmable execution and a shared data layer. The trade-off is that permissioning changes trust and governance: someone must define who can participate, how upgrades occur and what happens when the network is unavailable. A fast benchmark does not settle those governance questions or prove that the system is suitable for every regulated market.

Performance comparisons are only as good as their baseline. LayerZero says the pilot was around 45 times faster than a referenced layer-two EVM setup. That is useful for the experiment's stated conditions, but it should not be generalized to all blockchains or all workloads. Throughput can vary when orders are cancelled, modified or subjected to complex compliance checks. Latency, finality, settlement integrity and recovery from outages matter as well. A trading venue would also need to demonstrate that market data and order priority remain fair at high volumes.

Tokenized securities are often described primarily as a custody or issuance problem. But active exchange trading introduces difficult requirements around price-time priority, matching, market making and operations during volatile conditions. The partnership suggests infrastructure providers are taking those less visible tasks seriously. The promise of running an order book onchain is more auditable shared state, but that benefit depends on execution design and on who can inspect the records. A private test is a starting point rather than proof of an operating securities market.

The next milestones are independent testing, disclosure of methodology, resilient deployment across multiple participants and approval for any relevant regulated activities. Real trading volume and incident history would be more persuasive than repeating peak performance figures. If 21X and LayerZero can demonstrate those outcomes, this could become an important example of high-throughput financial infrastructure. Until then, the strongest conclusion is that the companies reported a promising benchmark under controlled conditions.

Primary source: 21X and LayerZero Labs joint release. For additional context, see NewsBTC’s report on the wider push toward tokenized securities venues.

BBC’s Near-Perfect Detective Thriller Teases Major Changes for American Remake

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Greer Riddell is a London-based film and television journalist writing about comedy, streaming and the evolving media industry in the UK and USA. She works at Collider, covering the latest in film, television and entertainment through interviews, features, reviews and industry coverage.

Her recent brand collaborations include HBO, Warner Bros., Netflix, Hulu, Sky, Disney+ and DC Studios. She has covered titles including Saturday Night Live UK, Rivals, Scrubs, Stuart Fails to Save the Universe, Ludwig, Supergirl and Stranger Things.

Her recent interviews include Florence Pugh, Mike Faist and Christopher Abbott for Netflix's East of Eden, Taika Waititi and Jenna Ortega for Klara and the Sun.

David Mitchell has made an unlikely detective out of a socially awkward, semi-reclusive puzzle setter in Ludwig, the BBC's hit murder mystery from Mark Brotherhood. With its combination of soft murder, a postcard-perfect British city and Mitchell in a role he seems born to play, the series has become one of the UK's most recent successes. Now, it's getting an American counterpart.

Kenton Allen, executive producer of Ludwig and CEO of Big Talk Productions, the company behind the series, spoke to Collider ahead of the Season 2 finale arriving on BritBox. He gave an update on what murder mystery fans can expect from Rabbit, the U.S. adaptation being developed for ABC by writers Humphrey Ker and Megan Ganz. But with the original series already finding an audience across North America through BritBox, why make an American version at all? And how much of Ludwig will make the journey across the Atlantic?

John Taylor lives a quiet, highly ordered life in his mother's house, rarely venturing beyond his familiar routines. He prefers the company of his books and puzzles, publishing his work under the professional pseudonym Ludwig. His identical twin brother, James, on the other hand, is a DCI in the Cambridge police force, married to Lucy (Anna Maxwell Martin), with whom he has a son, Henry. When James mysteriously disappears following his involvement in a murder investigation, Lucy turns to John for help finding him. Reluctant to get involved, John is eventually persuaded to take his brother's place at the police station, impersonating a detective while trying to establish what happened to James. However, his extraordinary ability to recognize patterns and solve complex puzzles proves surprisingly useful when it comes to solving murders, and he soon discovers that he's rather good at a job he has no qualifications for.

Britain makes more detective television than any country has any business making, and somehow almost all of it is good. Here is the one you belong in, what it says about you, and the case for it being the best of the four.

You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

The first season sees John navigate the increasingly complicated business of maintaining his brother's identity while investigating the circumstances surrounding his disappearance. By the Season 1 finale, the need for the impersonation has come to an end, but John's involvement with the police force doesn't. In Season 2, he continues helping the Cambridge detectives as a crime consultant, using his talents to solve a new murder each week while the wider mystery of James's whereabouts remains unresolved.

Alongside Mitchell and Maxwell Martin, Ludwig brings together familiar faces from British television for its weekly murder mysteries. Season 1 featured Sir Derek Jacobi as teacher Mr. Todd and Felicity Kendal as Lady Camilla Bright, while Season 2 welcomed Stephen Merchant as snooker player Danny Sloane in Episode 4. Rebecca Front and Doon Mackichan also appear as sisters whose mother is murdered at the theater. These guest appearances have made Ludwig a vehicle for seeing nostalgic names from British comedies in a completely different genre.

The show's success has extended well beyond its initial premise. According to the BBC, Ludwig Season 2 became 2026's biggest scripted launch across broadcasters and streamers to date, attracting 9.1 million viewers over 28 days. It also performed strongly on BBC iPlayer, where the opening episode attracted 5.5 million viewers, making it the second-biggest audience for the opening episode of a BBC comedy this year. The series has already been renewed for a third season, alongside a Christmas special. With those numbers, Ludwig has firmly established itself as a successful British detective series. So why does it need an American makeover?

The answer begins with understanding what makes Ludwig so appealing to international audiences, and why Allen believes some elements of the original are particularly well suited to export. Murder mysteries are hardly new for American television, with shows like Columbo and Monk helping establish the murder-of-the-week format long before John Taylor arrived in Cambridge. "There are some examples in the States now of this, and our inspiration for Ludwig," explains Allen.

However, he also believes the British setting is a significant part of what makes the series so distinctive. The production deliberately chose Cambridge as its backdrop and that sense of place is part of what Allen describes as "exportable UK" appeal. British murder mysteries have a long history of finding international audiences, and the combination of historic locations, familiar genre conventions and distinctive British talent gives them a particular appeal overseas. As Allen puts it, "Brilliant actors, brilliant writers, full stop. Our on-camera and off-camera talent is world-class." While the British setting and production talent might be part of the attraction, neither is necessarily something that can easily be recreated in an American adaptation.

Season 4 premiered in the U.K. last month.

Mitchell's portrayal of John Taylor is also central to the show's identity, and rather than attempting to find an American equivalent of Ludwig, the creative team wants to build something with its own personality. "Rabbit will be a brilliant cousin to Ludwig," Allen says. He believes the differences between the two countries will provide enough material to make the new series a distinct proposition, pointing to "where it's set and what the attitude is towards crime and crime prevention in North America" as important differences. "Our police don't carry guns," he jokes. "There's lots of cultural differences that I think will make an interesting companion piece to Ludwig."

Allen's thinking is that Rabbit should offer something familiar to fans of the original without becoming a direct copy. He points to the American adaptation of The Office as an example of how a British television concept can find an entirely new identity when moved across the Atlantic. Originally created by Ricky Gervais and Stephen Merchant, the BBC sitcom followed David Brent (Gervais) and the employees of the Wernham Hogg paper company in Slough, Buckinghamshire. When the concept was adapted for American television, Steve Carell took over as Michael Scott, managing the Dunder Mifflin paper company in Scranton, Pennsylvania. The very early episodes even borrowed directly from the British series, including the famous scene of Jim (John Krasinski) putting Dwight's (Rainn Wilson) stapler in jello. However, the American version quickly developed beyond those initial similarities. While the original British The Office consisted of 12 regular episodes and two Christmas specials, the American series ran for nine seasons and 201 episodes, eventually becoming a television institution in its own right.

Allen sees a similar opportunity with Rabbit. Rather than just reproducing the British series for a different audience, the intention is to take its central premise and explore what it could become in a different cultural setting. "I think that's what we're hoping to do with Rabbit, which is why it's called Rabbit and not American Ludwig," he explains. The important thing for Allen is that the new series has a reason to exist beyond the success of its predecessor. "It's seeing the fundamental setup through a different lens," he explains. "Nobody wants to make a facsimile of a show that we're making and is on air. So it has to have some reason to exist."

A large part of making that possible comes down to the writers involved. Rabbit is being developed by Ganz and Ker, two comedy writers with considerable experience across British and American television. Ganz previously worked on Community and Modern Family, earning an Emmy for her work, before becoming an executive producer on It's Always Sunny in Philadelphia and co-creating Apple TV+'s Mythic Quest alongside Rob McElhenney. Ker also has a connection to Mythic Quest, appearing in the series himself, and has worked closely with McElhenney through their shared involvement in football. Ker is the executive director of Wrexham AFC, the Welsh football club purchased by McElhenney and Ryan Reynolds. He subsequently became a familiar face to audiences through FX's Welcome to Wrexham and is also part of the writing team behind Saturday Night Live UK. The duo are also husband and wife.

Allen is particularly enthusiastic about what Ganz has brought to the project, praising her approach to making Rabbit feel connected to Ludwig without simply repeating it. He says that Ganz has found "a very cute, smart way of making a cousin rather than a copy." That distinction will be important for the adaptation. Ludwig already has an established audience, both in the UK and internationally, and its success on BritBox means American viewers can continue watching Mitchell's original portrayal of John Taylor. Rabbit doesn't need to replace that version or convince audiences that they should choose one over the other. Instead, the hope is that viewers who enjoy one will have another detective series to turn to, with its own characters, setting, and approach to crime.

Ludwig Seasons 1 and 2 are both streaming now on BritBox. ​​​​

Indiana Jones Star John Rhys-Davies Slams The Dial Of Destiny

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

James Mangold's 2023 adventure film "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny" was met with a lot of ambivalence back when it was first released, and star John Rhys-Davies, who has played the role of Sallah in two of the previous movies, wasn't fond of the way it turned out either. In a video interview posted on the Off/Frame Instagram account, Rhys-Davies admitted to being very disappointed in the film, largely because it focused too much on all-new characters, rather than on Indy (Harrison Ford), and himself:

"I'll be honest with you, I was very disappointed in it. The film is meant to be a nostalgia film. They filled the cast up with people we had never met before, so there can be no nostalgia, really. I found the scenes that I'd been promised in the thing were cut immediately after I signed the contract. I think it's a disappointing film."

"Dial of Destiny," despite catching up with Indiana Jones in the year 1969, and featuring Sallah in a small role, was more focused on the adventures of the irascible Helena Shaw (Phoebe Waller-Bridge), Indy's adult goddaughter and something of a con woman who sells artifacts on the black market. Helena is a great character, I'd say, but Rhys-Davies has a point in that the movie was a little too focused on her, and not on the nostalgic characters the audience paid money to see.

And as an actor, of course, he has every right to be upset about his part being reduced after he signed his contract to appear in the movie. Rhys-Davies doesn't go into detail about what larger role Sallah was to play in "Dial of Destiny," but in the finished film he certainly doesn't play a major role at all.

John Rhys-Davies also noted that Antonio Banderas appears in "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and that he wasn't utilized correctly either. Banderas plays a frogman named Renaldo whose screentime is very brief indeed. Regarding Banderas, Rhys-Davies said:

"And what is tragic, really, is ... I mean, there are a number of tragic things. I mean, Banderas, the most exciting actor of his generation. [...] I think he's a wonderful actor. But, really, what a terrible thing to do to him.

Some might defend the tone and the visuals of "Dial of Destiny," which are largely accurate to Steven Spielberg's Indy movies, but no one could argue that Antonio Banderas or John Rhys-Davies were used well. Perhaps the filmmakers didn't want to write a 2023 adventure story with two actors — Ford and Rhys-Davies — who were born in the 1940s.

It certainly could have been cheaper. "Dial of Destiny" was, according to some reports, a $419 million movie, making it possibly one of the most expensive movies of all time. This means that its worldwide box office total of just under $384 million didn't come close to turning a profit. /Film even saw the writing on the wall a week before the movie's release: "Dial of Destiny" wasn't going to make nearly enough.

Now, had the filmmakers scaled back on the film's action mayhem and caught up with an elderly Indiana Jones and Sallah who spent their time in libraries and slowly trekking into quiet caves, maybe you could have had something. Perhaps John Rhys-Davies could have played a much more central role. But then, an action-light Indiana Jones movie might be anathema to the nature of the character.

It has only been a few years since the release of "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and the film doesn't seem to have undergone any kind of major relitigation in that time. The weary ambivalence towards the legacy sequel still seems to be firmly in place.

Reviews of "Dial of Destiny" were generally good, but no critic seemed to be all that overwhelmed. /Film's review, by Lex Briscuso, was positive, giving the film an eight out of 10, and said that "the film has a fun plot that feels completely true to the sense of adventure the series has always brought to its audience."

Although Owen Gleiberman's review in Variety just as accurately called it "a dutifully eager but ultimately rather joyless piece of nostalgic hokum." "Dial of Destiny" was the first Indiana Jones movie not to be directed by Steven Spielberg, and the entire affair felt like a tired victory lap with many new figures juggling the baton. It didn't help that star Harrison Ford was almost 81 when the movie was released to the public. He was hardly the same action star he was in "Raiders of the Lost Ark," released 42 years prior, and wasn't as well-suited for the fighting and whip-swinging the character was better known for.

"Dial of Destiny" isn't a low-quality embarrassment, but it's also simultaneously too large and too small. It only ever felt like a means to milk more money out of 1980s I.P., and never a serious step in the evolution of the character. John Rhys-Davies wasn't the only one who was disappointed.

Trump says Jensen Huang told him Monday he didn't know paramour 'was a spy'

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump said he spoke to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Monday, and that Huang denied knowing that the woman he admitted having an extramarital affair with "was a spy" for China.

Trump told reporters at the White House that he was "amazed" by the allegations against Huang, which led Nvidia's board to initiate an independent investigation.

Trump suggested he had only recently learned of allegations that Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang was a Chinese spy.

"I didn't hear about it then, but a lot of people have heard about it, and Jensen totally denies that," Trump said.

"I asked him today because I did speak to him today just to see if he's feeling all right. 'How you doing?'" Trump said.

"And I asked him about that, and he ... said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," the president said. "I asked him that question: 'Did you have any idea that she was a spy?' He said, 'I had no idea that she was a spy.' "

The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that Alice Huang had been eyed by U.S. spy agencies "for possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China," and that "American officials had concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing."

The Times reported that about a year ago, Trump was briefed about the intelligence findings "and chose not to do anything with the information." The newspaper noted that Trump has been reluctant to disrupt chip sales by Nvdia, which are boosting the U.S. stock market.

On Friday, The Times reported that a memo that circulated last spring among top Trump administration policymakers "cited intelligence that a Chinese businesswoman who had a close relationship with Nvidia's chief executive was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security."

The Times said the memo reported that Alice Huang was recruited to collect intelligence on the Nvidia CEO "as part of a drive by Beijing's top spy agency to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China."

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump said Monday. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Trump on Monday said of Jensen Huang, "He's suffering greatly."

"I believe he's suffering greatly. I read his statement, and I believe he's suffering greatly, more so because of his family, his wife, who he loves, and his family. But he is definitely suffering."

Asked if he would bring up the issue involving Huang with Chinese President Xi Jinping when the two leaders meet next week at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, Trump said, "I don't think so."

"I mean, maybe I'm sure President Xi will ask me about it," Trump said. "I just know Jensen very well. I just think he's a very honorable, honorable man. I mean ... he built a great company, American company, and I think he's a very honorable guy, and I feel very confident that he'll be in good shape."

Trump's comments came as a Los Angeles law firm filed a class-action shareholder lawsuit against Nvidia and Huang in federal court in San Jose, California.

The Rosen Law Firm's suit alleges the defendants made false or misleading statements about his affair with Huang and Nvidia's relationship with Megaspeed, which presented a material risk to the company's reputation and business prospects.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-announces-10b-awards-rural-health-transformation-programs-second" hreflang="en">Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Trump administration announces $10B in awards for Rural Health Transformation Program’s second year" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration announced Monday funding for the second year of the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), with $10 billion in awards slated for all 50 states.

The U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) announced awards through the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

“President Trump made a historic investment in rural America, and HHS is putting those resources to work in every state,” said Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., HHS secretary, in a statement. “This next $10 billion will expand access to care, strengthen the rural health workforce, improve prevention, and help states build solutions that fit the needs of their own communities. We are giving rural America the resources and flexibility to build stronger health systems that deliver better care and better outcomes.”

Under the statute, half of the $10 billion-per-year distribution is split between the states evenly. The remaining half is determined by the CMS, with a portion of second-year funding being determined by states’ progress in implementing approved RHTP plans.

Federal officials say the annual review “strengthens accountability” for the investment.

Every state submitted applications to the federal government last November outlining plans to improve rural healthcare through their share of $50 billion in funds from the program, with CMS announcing first year awards in December.

Similar to fiscal year 2026, Texas and Alaska will receive the largest amount of funding as part of the program. Texas is set to receive $281,976,026, while Alaska will receive $272,653,149.

Other top recipients were Montana ($239,740,669), California ($231,105,823), Oklahoma ($227,097,543), Kansas ($225,896,117) and Georgia ($221,731,766).

New Jersey will receive the lowest amount of funding at $142,338,224, followed by Connecticut ($153,652,078), Rhode Island ($158,171,967), Massachusetts ($158,392,692) and Delaware ($160,294,521).

“Rural Americans have life expectancies three years shorter than their urban peers, but President Trump’s Rural Health Transformation Program is funding the historic investments necessary to close that gap,” said Mehmet Oz, M.D., CMS administrator, in a statement. “States have already put these resources to work bringing better care closer to home for their rural communities, and CMS looks forward to helping them build on that progress in the coming year.”

→ Baca artikel asli

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/alignment-healthcare-sues-cms-over-its-2027-medicare-advantage-star-ratings" hreflang="en">Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elevance Health is the latest insurer to sue the feds over its performance in the latest round of star ratings for the Medicare Advantage program.

The company sued the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services in District of Columbia federal court on Friday, arguing that the 2027 star ratings calculations violated the Administrative Procedure Act in using 11 measures that it says were adopted unlawfully.

In the lawsuit, the company said that the harm from including these measures is "concrete and substantial." If they were excluded, it would increase the scores for 12 of its Medicare Advantage contracts and generate $516 million in additional quality bonus payments.

"Their inclusion therefore does more than violate statutory requirements in the abstract: it depresses Elevance Health’s ratings and reduces the funding available for the following payment year," per the lawsuit. "That funding could support broader supplemental benefits, lower premiums and cost-sharing, and improved care coordination."

"CMS’s unlawful methodology puts those resources out of reach," the company said.

In addition to the financial impacts, Elevance argues that the star ratings scores can also lead to reputational damage that makes it harder for them to compete in the MA market. It also provides prospective enrollees with inaccurate information, the company said.

"Beneficiaries cannot make informed coverage choices when the government’s own comparison tool conveys a distorted assessment of the plans available to them," Elevance said.

"An artificially depressed rating does not merely place Elevance Health at a competitive disadvantage; it undermines the very comparison the program is supposed to enable," the company continued. "A rating lowered by unlawful measures presents beneficiaries with a misleading signal of relative quality, rather than a lawful basis for distinguishing among competing plans."

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that looked different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites the Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

I bought my first house in 1987. Each time I bought a house, I was nervous before signing the contract and for probably the first few months after moving in. The Top 5 Biotech Stocks I'd Buy Right Now Without Any Hesitation

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These are among the best the industry has to offer.

Biotech stocks have performed well in 2026, but things could change fairly rapidly. The industry is notoriously volatile, after all, and there are significant regulatory issues that may weigh on biotech companies. However, regardless of what happens over the next year or two, there are several biotechs with attractive prospects that are worth investing in for the long haul. Let's consider five of them.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX +0.85%) has long been the leader in the cystic fibrosis (CF) medicines market, and the company's core therapeutic area remains a meaningful growth driver, with no major patent cliff expected over the next 10 years. However, the company is also making strides elsewhere. Consider Vertex's efforts in kidney diseases. It is inching closer to an approval for povetacicept, a medicine for IgA nephropathy.

Its Phase 3 pipeline also features inaxaplin, an investigational drug for APOL-1-mediated kidney disease. The biotech boasts several recent approvals whose sales should ramp up over the next few years, and it has also diversified its pipeline. Vertex's dominance in its core market and progress elsewhere make the stock a buy.

While Vertex's growth comes primarily from a single therapeutic area, Amgen (AMGN +0.03%) has taken a different approach. In the third quarter, the company had 17 products that delivered at least double-digit year-over-year sales growth. It also had 17 medicines with at least $1 billion annual revenue run rate. That's impressive, and it helps explain why Amgen has been able to overcome recent major patent cliffs without too much trouble, including the one for denosumab, a medicine for several bone-related conditions.

The drugmaker's deep pipeline, which features MariTide -- a highly promising weight-loss medicine -- should enable it to extend its approved lineup. Finally, Amgen is also an excellent dividend stock, having increased its payouts every year since initiating them in 2011.

Gilead Sciences' (GILD -0.41%) portfolio of medicines features some of the best-selling HIV drugs, such as Biktarvy and Descovy for PrEP. The company still generates the bulk of its sales from this area, and one recent approval has helped strengthen the company's position. Gilead Sciences earned approval for Bixlenvo, an HIV pill. However, Gilead Sciences has also made headway in other areas, particularly oncology, in recent years.

Gilead Sciences is betting big on its cancer drug pipeline, which is larger than its HIV pipeline. And as it launches new products in this area, the company will have a stronger, more diversified lineup. Meanwhile, revenue and earnings growth remain strong for the company, and the dividend is rock solid, too. Gilead Sciences has increased its payouts by 74.5% over the past decade.

Exelixis (EXEL +1.25%) isn't the most popular biotech company. It is much smaller than all of the others discussed here. However, Exelixis has managed to become successful in a corner of the vast and highly competitive oncology market. The company's most important medicine, Cabometyx, has earned multiple indications across multiple cancer types.

Cabometyx will likely face generic competition in the U.S. by early 2030, but Exelixis is ready for it. The company could earn approval for zanzalintinib, an investigational medicine for colorectal cancer, next year. It will target additional indications while also developing new cancer medicines. Exelixis' position in oncology makes it an attractive biotech to buy.

Regeneron Pharmaceuticals (REGN +0.16%) has encountered stiff competition for Eylea, a medicine that treats several eye-related diseases, in recent years. This has harmed the company's financial results. However, the drugmaker is slowly putting this issue in the rearview mirror. Revenue and earnings growth have rebounded, largely thanks to a new formulation of Eylea and Dupixent, a medicine for eczema. Regeneron's long-term plans feature both the new version of Eylea and Dupixent.

Dupixent may run into a patent cliff early next decade, but Regeneron will likely seek to protect its empire, notably by developing a high-dose formulation of Dupixent. The company also boasts an attractive pipeline of programs to diversify its lineup, including promising weight-loss candidates. Regeneron's comeback may just be getting started. It's still time to buy the stock.

Prosper Junior Bakiny has positions in Exelixis and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amgen, Exelixis, Gilead Sciences, Regeneron Pharmaceuticals, and Vertex Pharmaceuticals. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Trump says renewed Iran strikes ‘highly unlikely’ if talks progress but warns he could still strike

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump said he is "highly unlikely" to restart open war with Iran, but warned he could still strike the country as Washington tries to reach a deal with Tehran to end the conflict.

The now more than eight-month-old war, which stoked inflation and sent oil prices surging, has seen repeated bursts of fighting separated by ceasefires and peace talks for months. It was widely speculated that Trump might restart open conflict after the midterm elections, where his Republican Party lost badly to Democrats.

Trump's comments came during an interview with CNN, where he said restarting the war is unlikely as long as talks to constrain Iran's nuclear program and reopen the Strait of Hormuz make progress.

"It depends on whether or not they sign a very strong agreement," Trump said. "The answer is highly unlikely it's going to be necessary. They're a failing nation."

Trump, however, left the door open for additional strikes if he deems it necessary. The president reiterated his threats to strike bridges and power plants in Iran if the war restarts.

"I've got three aircraft carriers here. The other one just arrived. We have three. They're fully locked and loaded," the president said. "We have 38 bridges in our site that we can knock down in literally one hour."

An end to the war and reopening of the Strait of Hormuz would likely ease oil prices, though finding a truce has proven difficult. All previous attempts to end the war and reopen the strait have been met with disappointment.

Trump is likely to come under increased pressure to end the conflict, however, now that Democrats have won control of both the House and the Senate. Some Republicans are also likely to be eager for an end to the war, given the role high gas prices played in their drubbing at the ballot box.

The president has demanded Iran fully open the Strait of Hormuz and agree to never develop a nuclear weapon as part of any deal.

Later in the day on Truth Social, Trump posted that "Task Force Iron Gate is in full force and effect in the Islamic Republic of Iran."

"Coupled with history's greatest Naval Blockade, very little gets into, or out of, Iran!" he said.

Central Command announced Task Force Iron Gate on Saturday, describing it as an initiative aimed at "preventing the movement of advanced weapons systems, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials, and illegal revenue streams across Iran's land borders and the Caspian Sea."

The U.S. has maintained a naval blockade of Iranian ports for months.

Here's How Much AAA Says It Costs To Own A Car In 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When we consider whether we can afford a car, so much of the focus tends to be on the sticker price that we often forget that the costs don't stop once we've made our purchase. Small, everyday expenses that come alongside, like fuel, servicing, finance interest, depreciation, insurance, and tax, aren't always obvious, but the true cost of car ownership adds up over time.

With that in mind, the car valuation experts at AAA performed a study to investigate car ownership costs in 2026. It turns out that drivers are facing ongoing price increases, with the AAA 2026 Your Driving Costs study revealing that the annual cost of owning and running a new car has soared to $12,863, or $1,071.92 per month this year. That's up from an average of $11,577 or $964.78 per month in 2025.

At that rate, ownership costs can add 32.67% on top of the $39,376 sales-weighted average MSRP of new vehicles. The larger bills have been linked to surging fuel prices, faster depreciation, and higher finance interest rates. Still, your specific costs can vary greatly, depending on your car's fuel type.

Owning any car at all is getting more expensive, but the AAA study revealed something more fundamental: Electric cars cost more to own than their traditional fuel-powered and hybrid counterparts. That's largely because depreciation on EVs is worse than gas or hybrid cars. On top of that, those who drive electric cars face higher fees and finance costs. Where EVs tend to do better is in fuel expenses, as they typically cost 66% to 70% less to run than gasoline cars.

Looking at a set of average consumption figures, AAA found that regular-grade gasoline costs $4.152 per gallon on average, while electric cars charged at home averaged as little as 18 cents per kilowatt hour. EVs have seen smaller electricity rate increases, too. The AAA data shows electricity prices have jumped 7.8% since 2025, while gas costs have increased a shocking 31.8%.

Even so, owning and running EVs costs more than gas-powered models, due to higher fees, finance costs, and depreciation, with certain EVs said to depreciate about twice as fast as their gas-powered counterparts. That contributed to pushing EV ownership costs above gas-powered models across the medium sedan, compact SUV, and pickup truck segments.

Nowhere is that gap more pronounced than in the medium sedan segment, where gas-powered medium sedans cost $3,080 less to own (at $10,582 per year) than comparable EV medium sedans ($13,662). Hybrids are even cheaper (at $10,204) and cost the least to own in every category, including the medium SUV class, where EVs win back some ground against gas-powered models, costing $14,332 versus $14,431 for gasoline SUVs.

As a car owner, every mile you drive costs money, but it is entirely possible to keep costs down. One way to do this is to shop around for cheaper insurance. Compare quotes from providers, or even consider buying a car from one of the cheapest brands to insure.

If you are team gas-powered cars, getting a model with great fuel efficiency is just as important. It should be easy enough, seeing as gasoline cars are now much more efficient than they used to be, with some of the most fuel-efficient models released over the years rated to return as high as 58 mpg.

You'll also want to consider depreciation. It is one of the single largest ownership costs and can add a weighted average of $4,422 per year to the cost of running some popular models, according to the AAA study. Toyota, Subaru, and Honda are some of the best brands for value retention, but if you're after something more premium, there's always Lexus. It's the luxury car brand with the best resale value, according to CarEdge.

However, maintenance and spare parts are usually more expensive for luxury marques than mainstream brands like Toyota or Honda. Speaking of which, staying on top maintenance is yet another important part of vehicle ownership that can help keep costs low. Not only does it help prevent larger bills down the road, keeping up with car maintenance also provides a strong service history that supports resale value when it comes time to sell.

Why Singapore Airlines Uses Boeing 787-10s On Short Regional Routes

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Boeing built the 787-10 Dreamliner as the largest Dreamliner variant with the shortest range. That makes it well suited to high-frequency, high-demand medium-length routes. Yet, with a maximum range of 7,500 nautical miles (13,890 km), it is still capable of traditional long-haul missions. Singapore Airlines takes its 787-10s in a different direction, using them to fly bizarrely short routes, typically the domain of narrowbody jets.

Singapore Airlines uses its Dreamliner in an unexpected way for several reasons. The 787-10 is a modern jet that replaced the carrier's older widebody aircraft. It has a high-density configuration ideal for ferrying a large number of passengers. It is also fuel-efficient and versatile, making it cost-effective on a range of routes. The answer is also in Singapore Airlines' current fleet, which lacks capacity for short-haul routes with other jets.

Cirium data reveals that Singapore Airlines will fly the 787-10 on 26 routes throughout November. This is for a total of 1,129 operations and over 380,000 seats, with each jet having 337 seats available onboard. The majority of these 26 routes are regional, coming in at less than a third of the 787-10's range.

The table below includes details on the ten shortest 787-10 routes scheduled for November. All of these routes originate at Singapore Changi Airport (SIN):

I Gusti Ngurah Rai International Airport (DPS)

Guangzhou Baiyun International Airport (CAN)

Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport (DAC)

These routes have impressive frequency, showing that Singapore Airlines uses its Dreamliners to move many people over short distances. The fifth-shortest route in November is the carrier's most frequent route that uses the Dreamliner, with 120 monthly frequencies from SIN to MNL. This route is 1,474 miles long and produces almost 60 million available seat miles. That is more than all but two of the routes.

Another exceptionally frequent route included is the third-shortest, taking passengers 876 miles (1,410 km) between SIN and BKK. The airline will complete this route 106 times in November, offering 35,722 seats. This produces over 31 million available seat miles. Meanwhile, it will run daily operations on the shortest route, traveling just 675 miles from SIN to SGN. The over 10,000 seats produce 6.8 million available seat miles.

The 787-10's position within the Singapore Airlines' fleet shows sound logic behind this usage. The carrier lacks narrowbody options that would traditionally operate these short services. Its only single-aisle aircraft are its 26 Boeing 737 MAX 8 jets. These planes have a total capacity of 154 seats split between economy class and business class. This is less than half the 337-passenger capacity of Singapore Airlines' 787-10s. This means the airline's 737 fleet has a total capacity of 4,004 passengers, compared with 9,436 for its Dreamliners. The 787-10 can simply take more of a chunk out of the high-capacity needs within a region packed with huge cities.

The table below shows Singapore Airlines' current fleet, using Planespotters.net data, revealing the position of the 787-10 within it:

On the other hand, all the carrier's widebody aircraft are overengineered for short-haul routes. The most numerous widebody aircraft in its fleet is the A350-900. This jet has a massive range of 8,500 nautical miles (15,750 km), which can extend to 9,700 nautical miles (17,964 km). As a result, the A350-900 has proven effective on long-haul and premium-dense routes. +

The airline often flies its MAX aircraft alongside its larger widebody services to ensure appropriate capacity is deployed on various routes.

Singapore Airlines' Dreamliner configuration has been built with high-frequency and high-capacity needs in mind, making it ideal for moving many people between cities in the Southeast Asia region. Premium passengers have access to 36 business class seats in a 1-2-1 configuration, while the rest of the aircraft is taken up by a massive 301-seat economy cabin. Singapore Airlines has no premium economy class available on this jet. Just one row in this economy cabin is marked as extra legroom.

The table below shows the dimensions of the seats in each of the two cabins, making use of Seat Maps data:

20 inches (51 cm) - expands when armrests are lowered

36 seats is admittedly a substantial business-class cabin, which seems to go against the high-capacity, short-haul philosophy this article has focused on. However, Singapore Airlines uses a staggered layout that squeezes in these seats in a relatively small area. Furthermore, although these seats are lie-flat, they have a shorter pitch and narrower width than what business-class passengers would usually expect.

Despite the prevalence of routes under 2,000 miles (3,200 km), there are still some routes that get closer to the typical way in which carriers use widebody aircraft. In November, Singapore Airlines will fly its 787-10s over 3,000 miles (4,830 km) on four routes. These will be Adelaide Airport (ADL), Tokyo Narita International Airport (NRT), Chubu Centrair International Airport (NGO) in Nagoya, and Osaka's Kansai International Airport (KIX).

Notably, only one of these routes takes the 787-10 outside Asia, and the longer routes within Asia largely reflect Japan's relative isolation from the rest of the continent. The longest of these four routes is to ADL, covering 3,351 miles (5,400 km). Singapore Airlines will complete this route 52 times in November, for a total of 17,524 seats and 58.7 million available seat miles. In 2024, Singapore Airlines marked 40 years of its presence in the city. The airline first flew there in 1984, 18 months after ADL's international terminal opened, using a 747. Along with the 787, Singapore Airlines also uses the A350 for this route, which began in October 2023.

The highest-frequency route, over 3,000 miles, is to KIX, with 90 frequencies across November. This produces 30,330 seats at an average distance of 3,040 miles (4,900 km), producing over 92 million available seat miles. That total is the highest for any Singapore Airlines Dreamliner route.

But it is not the large variant's longest passenger-carrying route of all time.

Relying on a single aircraft type to operate a specific route will always leave an airline vulnerable. This could become a problem for Singapore Airlines with a Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) airworthiness directive coming into effect next month. It covers all 787 variants and was instituted because sealant defects could create a spark inside the jet's fuel tanks during a lightning strike. "The unsafe condition, if not addressed, could result in development of an ignition source within the fuel tank volume," states the FAA order.

The prevalence of this issue and the extent of the problem it will cause remain unclear. The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) says that 60 of the 170 US-registered 787s fall under the directive, requiring safety inspections. Singapore Airlines' Dreamliners do not fall under this directive, but could be affected if a problem exists for Dreamliners worldwide. The FAA says that impacted jets need sealant inspections where the wings meet the fuselage, along with adhesion checks. FAA predictions put the work for each jet at 34 hours, costing $2,890 per jet. Further costs will be incurred where repairs are required. With over 1,100 Dreamliners worldwide, the problem could cause substantial disruption across global aviation.

Elsewhere in Singapore Airlines' widebody fleet, Boeing problems are causing other issues. Most notably, the carrier has an outstanding order for 31 Boeing 777X jets, placed in June 2017 and February 2021. It has no options for additional purchases. This aircraft family has been long-delayed by certification issues, although Boeing insists that certification and deliveries will finally come in 2027. Given the aircraft's huge size, its arrival should further cement the need for Singapore Airlines to use its Dreamliners on short, high-capacity routes.

Here's What the Average Man's Social Security Benefit Looks Like by Age

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Men's average retirement benefit runs $409.85 higher monthly than women's, a gap rooted in lifetime earnings with survivor benefit implications.

Men's average Social Security benefits run higher than women's at every age. This breakdown lays out the specific figures across the early retirement years based on the most recent data snapshot.

As of December 2025, the average retirement benefit among men is $2,282.28 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA). The average for women is $1,872.43, which leaves a monthly gap of $409.85.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your 35 highest-earning years. These wages are adjusted for inflation and run through a formula designed to replace about 40% of your pre-retirement income. Most retirees need a much higher share of their prior pay to cover their costs.

These averages represent a snapshot of current beneficiaries, including those who claimed early and those who waited for higher payments. As of December 2025, the monthly averages show consistent differences between genders across the board.

The higher average for men reflects historical differences in lifetime earnings and years spent in the paid workforce. While individual work histories vary, these broader patterns shape the current benefit landscape across every age group.

A man who is 65 today is expected to live about 18 more years, while a woman is expected to live about 21 more years, according to the SSA. This means that men's Social Security benefits typically cover a larger proportion of their retirement costs than women's, who have to pay for more years of living expenses with smaller checks.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027. The above figures don't yet reflect that increase, which will add about $91 a month to the $2,529.62 average for 70-year-old men. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later.

Married people have the option to claim a spousal benefit on their partner's work record while their partner is still alive and a survivor benefit after their partner's death. You only get these checks if they're worth more than your own retirement benefit.

Since women have historically had fewer years in the workforce and lower average earnings, they're more likely to claim spousal and survivor benefits, which means married men also need to think about how their claiming decision could affect their spouse.

Your partner cannot claim a spousal benefit until you are receiving your retirement checks, so your claiming age may affect theirs. Talk your options over with your spouse and make sure you're both on the same page about when each of you plans to sign up. If one of you wants to change things up down the road, keep the other in the loop and adjust your retirement plan accordingly.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.
