Here's the Average Woman's Social Security Benefit by Age

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Women's average Social Security benefit runs about $410 less monthly than men's, a gap rooted in earnings history and workforce participation.

Social Security benefits for women average less than those for men across every age group. This report lays out the specific dollar amounts as of December 2025, the most recent data available, to show the current size of that gap.

The average benefit among women is $1,872.43 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA). That figure is $409.85 lower than the $2,282.28 average monthly benefit for men.

The table below shows the average monthly payments for retirees aged 62 to 70. These figures reflect the amounts as of December 2025 and illustrate the consistent difference in checks between men and women.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your highest-earning 35 years. It adjusts those earlier wages for inflation and runs the results through the primary insurance amount (PIA) formula. The persistent gap in benefit amounts between men and women reflects historical differences in lifetime earnings and fewer years spent in the paid workforce.

These payments are designed to replace only about 40% of your pre-retirement income, which is well short of what most retirees need to cover their costs.

Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later. Workers who claim at 62 accept a reduction in their monthly check, while those who wait until 70 earn delayed retirement credits worth 8% per year.

While women receive lower average monthly checks, those benefits typically have to stretch over a longer period. A woman who is 65 today is expected to live about 21 more years, compared to about 18 more years for a man, according to the SSA. This means the average woman needs her retirement income to last roughly three years longer than the average man.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027 to help these checks keep pace with inflation. For a 70-year-old woman receiving the average $2,024.08 benefit, that adjustment adds about $73 per month to that figure. The dollar gap is widest at the oldest ages in the table, and spousal and survivor benefits are part of the picture for many women.

The above information focuses on benefits for retired workers, but many women claim spousal or survivor benefits based on their partner's work records instead. This could give them more money than they'd qualify for on their own.

Spousal benefits are the benefits you're eligible for on your partner's work record while they're still alive. They're worth up to one-half of the amount your partner qualifies for at their FRA. You will only be able to receive this amount once your spouse is claiming their retirement benefits.

Survivor benefits are the benefits you're eligible for on your partner's work record after they pass away. These are worth up to 100% of the amount your spouse was eligible for or receiving at the time of their death. You will only get this amount if it's larger than your own retirement benefit.

Contact the Social Security Administration to learn more about whether you qualify for these benefits and how much they could be worth to you.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

American Airlines Updates Vouchers For Stranded Passengers—But Tells Employees There May Be Fewer Hotel Choices

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

American Airlines is updating how stranded passengers get hotel rooms, meal credits and rides during flight disruptions. An internal November memo I reviewed lays out automatic offers for eligible overnight delays and a new way for agents to arrange Uber rides without making passengers download the app. It also warns that employees may see fewer hotels to choose from. Getting passengers out of the customer service line and into a room sooner is great for getting sleep. On a recent American Airlines flight from New York to Austin that was cancelled after a long delay I didn’t even bother getting into the long line at the gate for a room voucher, I just booked something myself nad went on my way. What American Is Changing The update covers American’s Hotel, Meal and Transportation system, which employees call HMT. October 26: standalone meal credits. Agents can issue food and beverage credits for individual reservations affected by qualifying delays, cancellations and missed connections. The system uses flight information to evaluate eligibility and tells agents what qualifies. October 27: automatic offers for eligible overnight delays. The system sends hotel offers and applicable food and beverage credits when its eligibility and automation criteria are met, reducing the need for an employee to initiate the offer. Planned Uber Central rollout. At participating airports, authorized agents would arrange a ride on the customer’s behalf through the same system. The passenger receives a text message or WhatsApp link to start the ride, without downloading Uber. This should now be available but I haven’t seen passenger reports of the broadly available rollout yet (though customers reported it in limited release previously). Hotel inventory changes, date undetermined. American is changing the hotel inventory feed and selection process. Employees are told they may see fewer hotel choices. Automatic Vouchers Already Exist American has been working on this for years. I covered the electronic hotel and meal system in 2019, when agents still had to qualify customers and trigger offers. American was already describing automatic hotel, meal and transportation vouchers in 2025. This spring, they added meal issuance for an entire flight at once. In June, automatic meal vouchers expanded beyond elite members to all AAdvantage members. American’s January app changes already brought disruption assistance together: Their published customer service plan promises, upon request, an approved hotel for an overnight disruption they cause away from your city of residence, transportation there and back, and a food and beverage credit after a qualifying delay of at least three hours. The standard meal voucher I’ve been covering is $12. American recently renamed it a “food/beverage credit” because calling it a meal voucher led passengers to expect it to pay for a meal. How The Uber Change Works American already offers Uber vouchers. The change lets an agent arrange the ride for the passenger. Uber Central supports rides paid for by the business, with trip details sent to the customer. However, American’s instructions still put the hotel shuttle first, followed by an Uber voucher. Uber Central is for exceptions when those options don’t meet the customer’s needs. One passenger reported being at Washington National when agents refused Uber vouchers because a hotel had a shuttle even though it wasn’t running at 1 a.m. Fewer Hotel Choices Isn’t Great The memo’s most concerning sentence is: Frontline teams may see fewer hotel choices displayed than they are accustomed to seeing. Being able to choose a hotel matters. I’ve covered passenger complaints about unsuitable rooms and missing shuttles. Here’s a passenger who said the hotel floor was so dirty they spread sheets over it. If American can’t provide an approved hotel voucher, its customer service plan promises reimbursement of reasonable hotel costs. Its conditions of carriage also say reimbursement isn’t guaranteed when you book directly without written authorization. Credit card trip protections may reimburse required hotel overnights as well. Getting passengers out of the customer service line and into a room sooner is great for getting sleep. On a recent American Airlines flight from New York to Austin that was cancelled after a long delay I didn’t even bother getting into the long line at the gate for a room voucher, I just booked something myself nad went on my way.

The update covers American’s Hotel, Meal and Transportation system, which employees call HMT. October 26: standalone meal credits. Agents can issue food and beverage credits for individual reservations affected by qualifying delays, cancellations and missed connections. The system uses flight information to evaluate eligibility and tells agents what qualifies. October 27: automatic offers for eligible overnight delays. The system sends hotel offers and applicable food and beverage credits when its eligibility and automation criteria are met, reducing the need for an employee to initiate the offer. Planned Uber Central rollout. At participating airports, authorized agents would arrange a ride on the customer’s behalf through the same system. The passenger receives a text message or WhatsApp link to start the ride, without downloading Uber. This should now be available but I haven’t seen passenger reports of the broadly available rollout yet (though customers reported it in limited release previously). Hotel inventory changes, date undetermined. American is changing the hotel inventory feed and selection process. Employees are told they may see fewer hotel choices. ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Powers $31M in Historic Box Office Launch Against Subdued ‘Cat in the Hat’

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The sequel to 2023's 'Godzilla Minus One' made noise as 'Ramayana' and 'Wild Horse Nine' also debuted.

Godzilla Minus Zero is king of the box office in its opening weekend as the sequel feature tallies the biggest launch in its distributor’s history with nearly three times the debut of predecessor Godzilla Minus One.

Hitting 3,254 North American theaters from GKids and Toho, Godzilla Minus Zero conquered $31.6 million domestically for the weekend. This is being touted as the highest-grossing live-action foreign-language opening at the box office since 2004’s Hero launched with $17.8 million. (It’s behind the debut of last year’s animated Demon Slayer: Infinity Castle with $70.6 million, while 2004’s The Passion of the Christ and its massive $83.8 million opening is not included in the category as an American-produced project.) Godzilla Minus Zero notches the highest-grossing opening weekend for a GKids release, topping the $13 million for 2023’s Oscar-winning The Boy and the Heron. Related Stories TV Druski Embodies Elizabeth Holmes in 'You Can See Everything' Spoof on 'SNL' Movies Danny McBride's 'G.I. Joe' Movie Lands 2028 Release From Paramount

Godzilla Minus Zero added $31.4 million internationally, including $17 million in Japan. Filmmaker Takashi Yamazaki‘s sequel to 2023’s Oscar-winning Godzilla Minus One earned an A CinemaScore from audiences and performed well above the distributor’s projected opening in the mid-$20 millions.

Ryunosuke Kamiki, Minami Hamabe and Min Tanaka star in the new movie that carries a reported budget in the mid-teens. It centers on postwar Japan dealing with attacks from the three-headed Ghidorah and the possibility of Godzilla’s return. Godzilla Minus Zero holds an impressive 90 percent critics approval on Rotten Tomatoes, along with a 96 from audiences, with The Hollywood Reporter chief film critic David Rooney calling it a “thrilling jolt of marauding monster action.”

The sequel is making a bigger footprint than Godzilla Minus One, which opened in December 2023 with $11.4 million domestically and reached $57.1 million for the run, adding $56.9 million internationally. The film rode its massive breakout success to become Japan’s first feature to win the best visual effects Oscar.

Overall, the weekend showed an increase of around 11 percent over last weekend, with the studios avoiding new releases for the Halloween frame, and a roughly 40 percent uptick from the equivalent 2025 weekend that saw the debut of 20 Century’s Predator: Badlands, according to FranchiseRe. This October had trouble establishing hits that can continue to play deep into the fall.

Warner Bros.’ The Cat in the Hat grossed a sleepy $20.5 million domestically from 4,003 North American locations, on par with the studio’s projection. The two previous animated Dr. Seuss adaptations had far higher debuts, with Universal’s 2018 The Grinch swiping $67.5 million in its opening, while that studio’s 2012 launch of The Lorax claimed $70.2 million. The studio hopes for word of mouth to help the $92 million-budgeted The Cat in the Hat play through the Thanksgiving holiday and into December after earning an A CinemaScore, with competition on the horizon from Disney’s Hexed on Nov. 25.

Dr. Seuss films historically haven’t made a big splash overseas, and this one has staggered its international release, with around half of the territories set for later debuts throughout the remainder of the year. The Cat in the Hat added $10 million internationally for the frame.

Directors Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni’s animated adaptation of Dr. Seuss’ classic 1957 children’s book centers on the titular feline being given a final chance to prove his worth by helping a pair of siblings. Bill Hader leads the voice cast that also includes Matt Berry, Quinta Brunson and Xochitl Gomez. The movie marks Warner Bros. Pictures Animation’s first title since Bill Damaschke took over as president and chief creative officer in 2023.

Critics have been a bit aloof on the film, as it holds a 46 percent Rotten Tomatoes score from reviewers and an 87 percent score from audiences, with THR critic Frank Scheck calling it “more taxing than enjoyable.” It’s faring better critically than Universal’s 2003 live-action The Cat in the Hat starring Mike Myers, which holds an 11 percent score on Rotten Tomatoes. That movie opened to $38.3 million domestically en route to a North American cume of $101 million against a $109 million budget.

Sony Pictures released Prime Focus Studios’ Ramayana: Rise of a Legend in 1,433 North American locations, with the film grossing roughly $8.6 million for the weekend and $12.1 million for the four days. It added $32 million in India and an additional $11.5 million from 37 overseas markets. Boasting the widest domestic launch ever for an Indian movie, director Nitesh Tiwari’s religious epic focuses on the life of Prince Rama, with the two-film project’s budget reported at $420 million and beyond. It holds a 76 score from critics on Rotten Tomatoes, along with an 89 from audiences.

Also debuting in the top five is Angel Studios’ Drummer Boy, with directors Ben and Joel David Smallbone’s historical drama grossing a strong $9.3 million from 2,850 North American locations. Earning an A CinemaScore, the faith-based musical focuses on an unlikely bond during the American Revolution and stars Joel David Smallbone in the title role. On Rotten Tomatoes, it has a 50 critics score and a 94 from audiences. Angel was uniquely positioned to open the film in a major way after having established itself with the faith-minded audience.

“It has been eight long years of failure and triumph to bring Drummer Boy to the screen,” Joel David Smallbone said in a statement to celebrate the opening. “So as you’d imagine, I find myself deeply impacted by the fact that this personal story has found its way into hundreds of thousands of your hearts.”

Academy Awards hopeful Wild Horse Nine opened in 1,100 North American theaters with an estimated frame of $6 million. John Malkovich, Sam Rockwell and Steve Buscemi lead writer-director Martin McDonagh’s comedic thriller about two CIA operatives who travel from Santiago, Chile, to Easter Island in 1973. The Searchlight Pictures release holds a 94 critics score on Rotten Tomatoes and 91 from audiences, with Rooney’s review describing it as “uniquely affecting.”

Likely rounding out the top 10 is Black Bear’s Wicker, which declined just 23 percent in its third weekend with $3.3 million for a domestic cume of $19.8 million, along with an additional $9.1 million internationally. Olivia Colman and Alexander Skarsgard lead directors Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer’s romantic fantasy that continues to build on enthusiastic word of mouth, holding a 94 critics score on Rotten Tomatoes and 92 from audiences.

Expected to hit just outside the top 10 is Burns & Co and Fathom Entertainment’s Jimmy, with the Jimmy Stewart biopic opening to an estimated $2 million from 1,470 North American theaters. Scheck’s review calls star KJ Apa “top-notch” but deems director Aaron Burns’ film “heartfelt to a fault.”

Another Oscar hopeful launched in platform release as A24 debuted Club Kid on six screens, with the Cannes standout grossing $390,583 for an average of $65,097 per screen. Making his feature directorial debut, Jordan Firstman also stars as a party promoter who learns he has a 10-year-old son. Set to expand into Thanksgiving, it boasts a sterling 100 score from reviewers on Rotten Tomatoes, with THR critic Richard Lawson heralding it as “winning and clever.”

Amazon MGM Studios releases I Play Rocky in seven locations, and it grossed around $345,000 for a per-screen average of $49,200. Anthony Ippolito stars as Sylvester Stallone in director Peter Farrelly’s making-of story for the 1976 classic Rocky. In his review, Rooney wrote that the movie — which holds an 88 Rotten Tomatoes score from critics — creates “a wave of warm nostalgia.”

Among holdovers, A24’s much-discussed Elizabeth Holmes documentary You Can See Everything looks to pull in around $8.8 million for the weekend as it jostles with Ramayana and Drummer Boy to round out the top five. The domestic cume for directors Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s feature stands at $79.7 million.

Next weekend brings the Johnny Depp studio comeback vehicle Ebenezer, along with How to Rob a Bank and Paper Tiger.

This story was first published on Nov. 7 at 8:43 a.m.

Tom Hardy’s Epic 6-Season Crime Series Is a Near-Perfect Netflix Binge

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

From HBO’s The Sopranos to Paramount+’s MobLand, dysfunctional mob families make for great television — but no other group of criminals is quite as cunning, conniving, and calculating as the Shelby family from Netflix’s Peaky Blinders. Starring Oscar-winning Cillian Murphy as the notorious Shelby ringleader Thomas “Tommy” Shelby, Peaky Blinders takes viewers back to Small Heath, Birmingham, in 1919, the town that would witness the Shelbys’ rise, peak, and eventual downfall.

Violence and bloodshed are expected in the series, but Peaky Blinders is just as much a family drama as it is a crime epic. Blood may be thicker than water, but Tommy is a businessman first and a family man second. Across six seasons, Peaky Blinders delivers no shortage of shocking plot twists to keep audiences hooked, made all the more surprising by the fact that many come from the people closest to Tommy.

Inspired by the real-life street gang, Peaky Blinders follows Tommy, fresh out of serving in World War I, as he returns to Small Heath with plans to turn his family’s small horse-betting operation into a legitimate business. His opportunity comes when his men stumble upon a crate of stolen military weapons, setting his bigger plans for the Peaky Blinders into motion. From blackmailing police authorities and taking territory from rival gangs to expanding into bigger cities, each season sees Tommy taking another step toward legitimizing Shelby Company Limited.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

As Tommy’s ambitions grow, so do the risks. Every season also follows the seemingly untouchable Tommy pushing his luck further, making increasingly questionable decisions to keep his empire growing. He doesn’t even hesitate to use his own family members as collateral, insisting that it’s all for a greater purpose. That's part of the appeal of Peaky Blinders: watching just how long Tommy can get away with his schemes before his luck finally runs out. When a man who has spent his entire life building an empire finds himself with nothing left, the question becomes whether he can rise from the ashes or if this is finally where his story ends.

Peaky Blinders has no shortage of seasoned actors bringing its characters to life. Besides Murphy’s cutthroat performance as the morally ambiguous and frighteningly intelligent Tommy, the rest of the Shelby family are just as gutsy as their leader, with some arguably even more so. From Paul Anderson as Tommy’s violent but troubled older brother, Arthur Shelby, to Helen McCrory as the wiser and more level-headed Aunt Polly Gray, each family member has a distinct personality that makes them fascinating to watch. Put them all in the same room, though, and their explosive chemistry makes audiences wonder how they haven't killed each other yet.

The series also boasts an impressive roster of recurring characters, some of whom become regular fixtures in Tommy's life while others only appear for a season or two. Even with limited screen time, these roles attract plenty of talent. Anya Taylor-Joy, Stephen Graham, and Adrien Brody are just a few of the big names who join the cast in later seasons. Then there's Tom Hardy as Alfie Solomons, the leader of a London-based Jewish gang. Appearing across five seasons, Hardy makes for one of Tommy's most entertaining counterparts, with their unpredictable alliance and rivalry providing some of the series' best moments.

Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren also star.

The original Peaky Blinders series ended in 2022, seemingly sealing Tommy’s fate after six seasons of chasing one impossible high after another. However, the story didn't end there. In March 2026, creator Steven Knight released a feature-film continuation, Peaky Blinders: The Immortal Man, set years after the original show's finale. Following its premiere, Netflix doubled down on the franchise by announcing a two-part sequel series focusing on Tommy's sons, Duke (Jamie Bell) and Charles Shelby (Charlie Heaton). The cast also includes Jessica Brown Findlay, Lashana Lynch, and Lucy Karczewski.

Set in 1953, the sequel follows the next generation of Shelbys as Birmingham rebuilds itself after World War II. A massive reconstruction project creates new opportunities, but everyone wants a piece of it. At the center of the action is Duke (Bell), now grown up and even more ruthless than when audiences last saw him in The Immortal Man, where Barry Keoghan played the character. Meanwhile, his half-brother Charles (Heaton) has cut ties with the Shelby family and the Peaky Blinders. But once a Shelby, always a Shelby. With a family that endures for all the wrong reasons, Peaky Blinders is a near-perfect binge for anyone who enjoys watching a powerful crime family rise to the top, only to slowly fall apart from within. If you're caught up on MobLand and you're tired of rewatching The Sopranos, it's time to take a journey to Birmingham next.

A gangster family epic set in 1900s England, centering on a gang who sew razor blades in the peaks of their caps, and their fierce boss Tommy Shelby.

Big-six Brighton? Table since Hurzeler took over illustrates sustained Seagulls success

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brighton have made a flying start to the season and the table since they appointed Fabian Hurzeler illustrates that their fine form is no flash in the pan.

The Seagulls sit second in the Premier League table, the closest challengers to Arsenal after the first 10 games.

Brighton have scored more goals than anyone else this season, and only the Gunners have conceded fewer.

Hurzeler’s success, though, goes back far beyond the start of the current season.

The German was appointed in the summer of 2024. Then a relative unknown and just 31 years old, Hurzeler replaced Roberto De Zerbi, who had led Brighton to their highest-ever finish – sixth – in 2022-23 but left the Italian left the AmEx after the Seagulls finished 11th in 2023-24.

This is the overall Premier League table since Hurzeler took charge in June 2024…

Under Hurzeler’s management, through two full seasons and the start of the current campaign, Brighton are sixth – equal to their highest-ever position.

The Seagulls have achieved this consistency all the while making a net profit in the transfer market over the last five years.

The only season in which they spent more than they made was Hurzeler’s first, when they invested £191million.

Even that spend, though, could yet yield a hefty profit. According to Transfermarkt, the players bought in that period who are still at the club this term have seen their collective value rise by £69million since their Brighton debuts.

Last season, Brighton made £56million in the transfer market and this summer they turned a profit of almost £11million before leading the pursuit of Arsenal and City who, between them in the recent window, had a net spend of more than £240million.

No clear winner in New Zealand election, as leaders prepare for weeks of talks

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

New Zealand Prime Minister and National Party leader, Christopher Luxon addresses party faithful in Auckland, New Zealand, Saturday, Nov.7, 2026. Alex Robertson/AP hide caption

WELLINGTON, New Zealand — New Zealanders will wait for weeks to discover who will lead the country after Saturday's election failed to deliver a decisive win for the right or the left, leaving fraught negotiations for a wafer-thin governing majority the only apparent path to victory for either side.

The tightest vote in years delivered a stinging rebuke to the major parties which have long dominated New Zealand politics, Christopher Luxon's center-right National party and center-left Labour led by Chris Hipkins, as voters threw more support behind minor parties on the ideological flanks than before. On Saturday's numbers, minor parties would enter negotiations for a governing deal with leverage to extract deeper policy concessions from a weakened National or Labour.

The near-deadlock was partly produced because voters, apparently weary of New Zealand's economic woes and apathetic about both Luxon and Hipkins, chose to propel a new entrant group to Parliament for the first time in years. Opportunity, led by 38-year-old business consultant and political newcomer Qiulae Wong, is unaligned with either bloc and Wong has said she could work with either side to form a government.

Wong's pledges of evidence-based centrism and an end to partisan bickering had on Saturday's results secured seven seats for Opportunity — enough to frustrate both left and right in a simple path to power. It was a staggering ascension for a party that made three failed attempts at Parliament before Wong became leader less than a year ago.

Other winners were harder to identify and on Sunday, "who won the election 2026" was the top trending Google search in New Zealand. So unclear was the result that parties won't begin negotiations to form a government until final tallies are released on Nov. 27, confirming the exact number of seats they've won.

With 2.8 million ballots counted on election night, the conservative incumbent government led by Luxon's National party appeared close to the slimmest possible return to power — by just one seat. However, Luxon didn't claim victory, instead striking a note of cautious optimism that he could negotiate a second term in power.

Speaking to reporters Sunday, Luxon blamed public dissatisfaction on global shocks which have buffeted New Zealand's trade-based economy, noting that other incumbent governments worldwide have faced voters' rancor over living costs.

Yet his opponent Hipkins, who was turfed from office in 2023 largely over discontent about the same economic issues, didn't appear to benefit on Saturday. The left-wing bloc appeared to fall short of a majority but was still hopeful it could triumph — though that seemed a more remote prospect.

Electoral officials were due on Sunday to begin counting nearly 400,000 so-called special votes, including from New Zealanders who live abroad, a process expected to take three weeks.

Those ballots have historically granted an additional seat or two in Parliament to left-wing parties, which could snatch the current razor-thin majority from the right, although such an outcome would likely leave the left struggling to form a majority too. If a government can't be formed, New Zealand could face a fresh vote.

Labour Party leader Chris Hipkins addresses party faithful in Upper Hutt, New Zealand, Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026. Mark Tantrum/AP hide caption

"I think everyone's over politics so let's all have a bit of a break for the next three weeks," Luxon told reporters on Sunday. He will lead a caretaker government until an outcome is reached.

Luxon suggested that National had a viable path to power and boasted that it had secured more seats in Parliament than any other group. However, Saturday night was bleak for his party.

Several of National's most senior lawmakers would exit politics on results so far, including the ministers of finance and defense, the speaker of parliament, and the attorney general. With the party's traditional governing partners winning increased support, Luxon faces a more strident list of policy demands to secure their backing.

Libertarian ACT won 13 seats and populist New Zealand First won 12. Both are led by canny veteran politicians, and detractors of Luxon, a former business executive who entered politics in 2020, said his dealings with them in the three years since he took office had highlighted his political inexperience.

The Opportunity Party leader Qiulae Wong poses for a portrait outside Parliament in Wellington, New Zealand, Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. Charlotte Graham-McLay/AP hide caption

New Zealanders don't vote for prime ministers. Instead they cast two votes, one for their preferred party and another for the candidate they want as their local representative.

The country has one house of Parliament and no states, so the central government holds ultimate sway over New Zealand's direction. Governments in the South Pacific nation of 5.6 million people are elected every three years.

National won just 29.21% of the preferred party vote as of Sunday, a collapse of its previous result, and secured its 42 seats entirely by winning local constituency races. Luxon, who unusually faced two separateconfidence votes among his own lawmakers this year, further slumped in popularity as leader close to election day.

However, Labour also won just 29.36% of the party vote, a result that appeared to suggest a second election defeat for career politician Hipkins. Despite winning about the same party vote percentage as National, Labour has secured only 36 seats on numbers so far because they won fewer local races.

Minor parties on the left saw bolstered support. The environmentally-focused Greens won 16 seats and Indigenous rights group Te Pāti Māori secured five.

The total number of seats in New Zealand's Parliament changes due to quirks in how seat allocations are calculated. There would currently be 131 lawmakers elected, with 66 seats required for a majority.

Luxon's conservative bloc would currently hold 67, a razor-thin margin likely to discomfit a leader managing a three-way government with two emboldened minor parties. However, the left bloc held only 57 seats before the historically left-leaning special votes were counted.

Even if the final tally boosted the left bloc's seats slightly, Hipkins would apparently need support from newcomer Wong to govern.

Before the election, Luxon repeatedly ruled out negotiating with Wong. He sidestepped questions on Sunday about whether his vanishingly slim lead had changed his mind.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

‘On par with what the DoD pursued during the Cold War’: Pentagon’s war stockpile plan hits a minerals wall

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Pentagon wants to stockpile enough critical minerals, fuel, and munitions on American soil to keep fighting a major war even if cyberattacks and sabotage knock out factories and power grids at home. One defense expert says the scale of the planned effort is “on par with what the DoD pursued during the Cold War.”

The minerals may be the hardest part. The U.S. produces less than 1% of the most common type of rare-earth magnet it uses, and China, which dominates the processing of many critical minerals, has kept American companies short even in peacetime.

The initiative, known as FORTRESS America, short for Federal Operations for Resilience, Troop Resources, Energy, Supply, and Survivability, was formally established on Sept. 30. It is designed to keep U.S. forces operating if a conflict disrupts domestic power grids and manufacturing supply chains, and it is the same program behind the Pentagon’s push for major bases to plan for 90 days without the grid.

Stockpiling materials isn’t unusual for the U.S. military, Bryan Clark, a senior fellow at the Hudson Institute, told Fortune. What distinguishes FORTRESS is its planned scale and its shift toward domestic reserves rather than overseas stockpiles.

During the Cold War, the Pentagon stored as much or more war matériel overseas, near anticipated battlefields in Eastern Europe or Northeast Asia, Clark said. FORTRESS instead focuses on protecting supplies inside the United States, reflecting growing concern that American factories could themselves become targets.

China could disrupt U.S. industrial production through cyber operations, drones, electromagnetic warfare, or attacks carried out by people inside the country, Clark warned. U.S. agencies have already warned that Chinese state-sponsored hackers had positioned themselves inside American critical infrastructure.

That concern has a recent precedent in Europe. On Dec. 29, 2025, cyberattacks hit about 30 renewable energy sites, a manufacturing company, and a heat and power plant serving nearly 500,000 customers in Poland. Polish officials linked the attacks to Russia’s Federal Security Service and said the attackers tried to destroy industrial systems, but the country avoided a major outage.

Building stockpiles on the scale Clark described runs into a stark industrial reality: America’s defense industry remains deeply reliant on foreign supply chains for its raw materials.

In 2025, the U.S. imported about $60 billion worth of critical minerals other than rare earths, including nickel, lithium, cobalt, graphite, and tungsten, according to the Peterson Institute for International Economics.

The gap is most acute in rare-earth magnets. U.S. demand for the most common type reached roughly 48,000 metric tons in 2025, while domestic sources supplied just 300 metric tons, according to Arthur D. Little data reported by Reuters. U.S. companies are on track to have capacity for about 5,000 metric tons by the end of this year, roughly a tenth of last year’s demand.

The shortage has already reached factory floors. Ford, No. 22 on the 2026 Fortune 500, idled its Chicago Explorer plant for a week in May 2025 when rare-earth supplies ran short.

China has repeatedly tightened rare-earth export controls, and American companies are still struggling to secure enough supply, Reuters reported on Nov. 6, citing U.S. officials. That day, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer pressed Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng to fully carry out Beijing’s commitments on rare earths and other critical minerals.

A major war could further disrupt overseas production and shipping routes, making it harder for the Pentagon to replenish its stockpiles.

To reduce foreign dependence, Clark said, the Pentagon is pursuing a dual approach: building raw-material reserves while funding domestic mining and processing capacity.

Tungsten, a stockpiling priority Clark identified, illustrates why building reserves takes more than government spending.

The U.S. hasn’t commercially mined tungsten since 2015, when cheaper imports undercut domestic production, according to Scientific American. Its net import reliance for the metal exceeded 50% in 2025, according to the U.S. Geological Survey.

In September, tungsten manufacturer The Elmet Group won a Defense Logistics Agency contract worth up to $2 billion to supply tungsten ores, concentrates, and sodium tungstate to the National Defense Stockpile. Only $150 million of that is guaranteed. The Pentagon also committed $450 million to Elmet, including about $150 million earmarked to restart the Springer tungsten complex in Nevada.

Yet Elmet said it won’t begin deliveries to the stockpile until additional supply becomes available. To avoid diverting existing tungsten from American manufacturers, the company plans to wait until new production comes online from investments in Nevada, Australia, and Spain.

The deal illustrates a problem FORTRESS could face across the board: signing supply contracts doesn’t immediately put minerals into government vaults.

The cost of FORTRESS also remains uncertain. A senior defense official told reporters on Oct. 20 that officials are still assessing costs, Air & Space Forces Magazine reported. The magazine estimated the broader initiative could cost “scores of billions of dollars, if not hundreds of billions.”

To help cover the bill, the Pentagon is exploring public-private partnerships, Dale Marks, assistant secretary of defense for energy, installations, and environment, told Breaking Defense.

Even with capital flowing, analysts warn that supply chain independence can’t be rushed.

“The Pentagon is unlikely to meet the goals in the near term,” Clark said, pointing to the structural difficulty of rapidly stockpiling both finished weapons and raw minerals.

Chris Berry, a minerals industry analyst, told Reuters it will “take many more years” to build the infrastructure needed to compete with China’s dominant supply chain.

The first test comes soon. Starting Jan. 1, 2027, federal rules require defense contractors to stop buying rare earths, magnets, and tungsten from China, Russia, Iran, and North Korea. Washington has repeatedly granted waivers because domestic supply falls short, Reuters reported in July.

For the Pentagon, building the physical reserves may prove far harder than announcing them.

If I Could Give My Younger Self 1 Piece of Investing Advice, This Is What It Would Be

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There's no substitute for experience, but it still helps to know some important rules of thumb.

If I could give my younger self one piece of investing advice, I'd find most of the material I'd need by reviewing my first-ever investment. My first stock was Lucent Technologies, which in 1996 was spun off from AT&T, and then bought by me at age 10 with all $500 of the birthday money I had saved up from prior years. Shortly after my purchase in late 1999, it began a long decline that saw its price fall 99.2% by September 2002.

The sting of that collapse kept me away from investing for about 15 years. So here's the investing advice that I would give to my younger self if I had to do things over again -- it's one central theme, and it has three parts.

This advice seems obvious now, but new investors still need to learn it.

Funneling all your capital into one stock means one failure can wipe out most of your portfolio. Per research conducted at Arizona State University, about 4.3% of the nearly 26,000 U.S. stocks included in the analysis accounted for nearly all of the market's wealth creation above Treasury bills from 1926 to 2016. That's an argument for owning a large and diversified basket of stocks via an index fund like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%).

The lesson extends to the types of assets you invest in, too. The traditional 60/40 stocks/bonds portfolio split is timeless because holding bonds can make up for underperformance that stocks experience under certain conditions, and vice versa. Today, adding some cryptocurrency into your portfolio is another way to diversify even beyond that.

If you invest in a lot of different companies, some of those investments will not play out.

According to a 2014 J.P. Morgan study, 40% of Russell 3000 index stocks from 1980 to 2014 ended up experiencing a catastrophic loss, which in this case is defined as a 70% or larger drop from the peak with little recovery afterward. In the case of my first investment, in the telecom industry, the catastrophic loss rate was 51%.

The bigger point here is psychological in nature. Expecting every one of your stock picks to work out is not reasonable, but investing requires us to be rationally hopeful each time. The truth is that failure in investing is inevitable, and vastly more common than success. Quitting after the first big failure, as I did, cost me years of compounding, and not having the proper context to process the defeat in a constructive way was especially deleterious.

So when an investment does poorly, diagnose whether the cause was bad luck, incomplete or poor analysis, or something else, and then fix that and move on to the next opportunity. And then if a different investment fails, repeat the process, again and again, until your results improve. Reading about past stock market crashes can also show you how much pain is normal.

I originally picked Lucent for my first investment because it was among the first companies I knew of in general -- at age 10, I wasn't a sophisticated investor, to say the least! Its original identity before the spinoff from AT&T was the legendary Bell Labs, one of the greatest research and development (R&D) organizations of all time, which even as a child I knew enough about to admire.

Still, I did not really have a firm understanding of why Lucent's stock was going to be worth more (or, in this case, less) in the future. I didn't really have an investment thesis. And while it's certainly a tall order to develop that understanding, especially as a new investor, it's the core of the art of investing in stocks, so my advice to my younger self would be to start grappling with these issues to the extent that it would be possible to do so at that point.

Before buying a stock, I'd tell my younger self to write two sentences: one on what the company sells, to whom, and why they buy it, and one on why its profits should be bigger in five years.

Even at age 10, I think I could've worked out those questions. And while that probably still wouldn't have actually saved me from investing in Lucent, I'm sure that I'd approach the questions with a bit more sophistication in the next investment. And improving your process over the long run is the heart of the battle.

Alex Carchidi has positions in Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Hilton Is Growing Faster Than Marriott, Hyatt And IHG

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hilton opened 182 hotels and 27,000 rooms in the Q3 2026, which works out to 23,000 net additional rooms and net unit growth of 6% from a year prior, according to its earnings release. Net income was $536MM, up from $421MM, and adjusted EBITDA was $1.058bn. Management and franchise fee revenue rose 9.5% which is the company’s core business model.

Comparing that to the other three big loyalty hotel companies, Marriott added roughly 20,000 net rooms and grew 4.6%. IHG reported net system growth of 4.9% after opening 12,900 rooms across 92 hotels. Hyatt posted net rooms growth of 3.8% over the trailing 12 months. The companies define growth a little differently, but Hilton leads on every version I can find, and it did so while adding more net rooms in a quarter than Marriott, the larger of the two companies.

More rooms does not necessarily mean higher earnings. Hyatt’s comparable system-wide RevPAR rose 7.2%, Marriott’s rose 6.5% worldwide and 7.4% in the US and Canada, while Hilton’s rose 5.8%, and IHG’s rose just 5.3%. Hilton ranks third of four on that list which starts to eat into its net rooms added lead. Hyatt’s caveat is its all-inclusive business, where Net Package RevPAR fell 2.5%, which matters because every big hotel brand wants your all-inclusive money right now. This, for me, probably deserves its own post if the drops continue into the fourth quarter compared with YoY.

Net profit confirms the rate growth relative to both peers and net rooms is more tied to RevPAR than absolute growth. Marriott earned $794MM in the quarter and $1.52bn in adjusted EBITDA, well ahead of Hilton. Hyatt’s net income was $58MM, or $122MM adjusted, which is considerably less but HYatt is far smaller (and underperforms.) I wrote last week that Royal Caribbean out-earned Hilton’s whole 2025 in a quarter, and the hotel sector is a reminder that growth and earnings are different races.

Marriott’s development pipeline hit a record of more than 634,000 rooms across over 4,200 properties, with 44% of those rooms under construction. Hilton’s pipeline is 543,100 rooms across 3,869 hotels in 135 countries, with nearly half under construction and more than half outside the United States. IHG’s pipeline is 353,000 rooms, up 3.2% and equal to 33% of its current system. Hyatt’s is about 158,000 rooms, up 12.1%.

Marriott has the biggest pipeline in absolute terms, Hyatt’s is growing fastest of the three that disclosed growth, and Hilton grew 5%. Conversions matter here too. Marriott said conversions (existing independent hotels or those being reflagged) made up over a third of signings and over 40% of openings in the first nine months, and IHG says development splits roughly evenly between new builds and conversions. Hilton opened its first Select by Hilton hotels this quarter, one of several newer brands it is leaning on to fill that pipeline.

As someone whose agency sells hotel rooms, both of these numbers are important but for different reasons. My clients and I prefer to see the rate creep slow or stop (rate regression might be a dream) with more stays. But more properties to choose from is good news too and increases competition.

For travelers, the bigger question is what all this growth does to loyalty programs especially when comparing Hilton vs Marriott. Marriott Bonvoy and Hilton Honors each span well over a million rooms, and their luxury brands include Waldorf Astoria, Conrad, St. Regis, Ritz-Carlton and JW Marriott. But with so many of the new rooms being conversions and limited-service hotels where elite status buys less, that can reduce the shine of the programs. Lounge access and complimentary breakfast (a daily food and beverage credit at most U.S. Hilton hotels) are what business travelers chase but lounges are almost gone completely, and those perks are thinner at a select-service property. Hilton offers elites a fifth night free on standard reward stays and waives resort fees on free nights, and Hilton points pile up quickly through its brand credit cards with American Express. Long term, more hotels should mean more places to redeem, not just more places to earn. But the question also becomes how important those advantages are, and with both Bonvoy’s accelerated status earning, and Hilton’s Diamond via credit card option, the true value of those benefits continue to diminish.

Hilton returned $879MM to shareholders in the quarter, $2.9bn so far this year, and projects about $3.5 billion for 2026. It also moved its stock listing to Nasdaq on November 5, 2026 under the same HLT ticker. IHG has completed $707MM of a $950MM buyback and expects to return over $1.2bn in 2026. Hyatt projects $375MM to $425MM in capital returns. Hotels are on a tear over the last couple of years and Hilton’s growth in room count demonstrates confidence in the market for the future.

Hilton is winning the room race and not the rate race, and Marriott is winning the profit race. I expect Hilton to hold the unit growth lead through 2027, but Marriott’s larger pipeline will make that lead uncomfortable. Hyatt will continue winning on rate while sitting out the growth sprint barring another acquisition which it has been drawn to as of late, which is exactly how a luxury-leaning brand should behave. Still, it’s impressive to see Hilton with such strong numbers for growing the brand.

‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Is King Of The Global Weekend Box Office With $63M, Towers Over ‘Ramayana’s $55M+ WW Opening

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Refresh for more…Toho’s lizard had no plans to be undone by a Hindu diety in a fight at the global box office as Godzilla Minus Zero roared $63M WW over Prime Focus Studio and Sony’s Ramayana: Rise of a Legend‘s $55.7M global, the bulk of that gross in India.

Ramayana had good fortune in Europe, where we hear it was No. 1 in the UK and Australia, trampling the Godzilla and Ghidorah, but the monsters reigned in Mexico in the No. 1 spot and most of Latin America (however not Brazil where a local comedy title was No. 1). Also, the marketing spend on these movies are very thrifty, often as low as $1M worldwide (not counting India). Word of mouth is very easy to spread on Indian features given how close-knit the communities are around the world. And Ramayana here is akin to making a movie about Jesus Christ or even on the level of Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey.

“There are distractions like religious services on the days right before the Diwali holiday, then right after people in India are more free to go to the movies,” says Box Office Guru’s Gitesh Pandya, “It’s similar to how Christmas Eve impacts the box office in the U.S., with people having more time to attend theaters after that date.”

Prince Rama broke local market opening records for a film from India, including the United Kingdom ($3.0M), Thailand($550K), the Netherlands ($240K), and South Africa ($75K).

With a fight likes this between a Prince and reptiles, it’s Imax who benefits the most, collecting $29M combined from largely both movies around the globe.

Godzilla Minus Zero in Imax reaped $21.3M, repping 34% of the pic’s worldwide opening and getting a new record for the biggest Imax global opening for a Japanese film. As we old, PLFs and Imax combined counted a 74% share of the pic’s $31.6M opening. Imax alone in North America repped 40% of the weekend’s ticket sales or $12.7M at 429 screens. Overseas, Godzilla Minus Zero played on 421 screens across 64 markets or $8.6M with Japan leading the way with $4M — easily the highest Imax opening ever in the market.

“With Godzilla Minus Zero, Takashi Yamazaki and our friends at Toho have delivered a tremendous feat of filmmaking with arresting visuals that somehow top its predecessor; we are proud to call this the first Filmed For IMAX release from Japan and delighted fans are overwhelmingly choosing to experience it in on our screens,” said Rich Gelfond, CEO of IMAX. “Japan continues to be among the biggest success stories across our global footprint, and we know this historic success will only create further opportunities for filmmaker partnership and network growth in this thriving marketing for cinema.”

The Namit Malhotra directed Ramayana saw $6M from Imax, repping 11% of the title’s global weekend, delivering the biggest global Imax opening ever for an Indian film. The Filmed for Imax release saw $2M in Indian, which was the biggest Imax opening ever in the market. There was another $1.4M in Imax box office from 32 international markets outside India. North America delivered $2.5M from 53 screens, accounting for a strong 20% of the film’s stateside bow.

Here's Why I'm Still Investing in the S&P 500, Even if a Bear Market Is Coming

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Buying before a market decline feels awful. But waiting indefinitely carries its own risks.

The thought is inescapable: If I invest today, what if the market drops tomorrow? It's a question that can give everyone pause. After all, no one wants to see their newly invested money lose its value.

Yet the alternative creates an even more dangerous trap: Once you start waiting, what could possibly convince you it is ever safe to buy? That's why, when dealing with money you won't need for many years, it's better to build a plan based on uncertainty than to keep waiting for the perfect entry point.

War. Economic turmoil. Apocalyptic forecasts. The reality is that these things come and go. Sometimes the market falls as a result. Sometimes it doesn't. In reality, an informed person can always find what may seem like a reasonable excuse to postpone investing.

Worse, this anxiety often shows up no matter which way the market moves: Rising prices can feel expensive; falling prices feel like a warning sign. Nevertheless, studies have made it clear: Those who put money to work in the stock market have consistently outperformed those who have tried to time the market or remained in cash.

One way to clear the psychological hurdle of investing in a falling stock market is to recognize that lower prices can offer benefits. First, they mean you can buy more shares.

For example, imagine an investor who puts $200 to work in the stock market each month, buying an index exchange-traded fund (ETF) valued at $100 a share. That results in two shares per month. However, if the index ETF falls to $80 a share, the same $200 investment buys 2.5 shares per month.

Granted, the earlier-purchased shares have lost value, and buying more doesn't erase that loss. However, for those buying with the intention of holding for decades, the lower price can help them accumulate many more shares.

In the long run, that will result in more assets, which will in turn generate added returns. In other words, it can help power the compounding mechanism that drives buy-and-hold investing.

This is the essence of dollar-cost averaging. By regularly setting aside money for investment, a person can remove the emotional guesswork of investing and, if the market declines, lower the average price paid.

Another reason some people fear investing into a downturn is that they are putting the wrong money to work in the wrong way. That is, investment money should be reserved for long-term goals that won't be interrupted by short-term challenges. Emergencies, bills, and near-term purchases should come from a separate pool of money.

That way, investment money can fluctuate without affecting a person's ability to make ends meet. Moreover, by having a separate cash reserve, a person won't be tempted to sell investments during a downturn to raise cash.

Lastly, it's important to establish the right investing routine. If $200 per month is too much, lower the amount; don't give up on the routine. If finding the right stocks seems overwhelming, start with an index fund that tracks the S&P 500 -- many are incredibly cheap, and buying shares has never been easier. Also, an S&P 500 index fund is a great way for investors to stay diversified, because the index itself spreads money across many large American stocks.

Is a bear market coming? Perhaps. Does that make me nervous about investing? No. That's not to say that a bear market drop wouldn't hurt. It's just that long-term investing requires the fortitude to ride out dips, corrections, and even bear markets.

The best investors are often the ones who can see downturns for what they are: opportunities for long-term investors to accumulate more shares at a lower price.

Mikel Arteta has praised Noni Madueke’s response to a difficult start to the season after the winger played another decisive role in Arsenal’s 1-0 victory over Hull City.

Martin Odegaard scored the only goal at the Emirates Stadium after 72 minutes, reacting quickest when Kostas Tzolakis saved Madueke’s effort.

Arsenal were far from their fluent best, but the victory moved the defending champions six points clear at the top of the Premier League.

As reported by Arsenal News, Arteta believes Madueke has transformed his performances after responding positively to limited playing time earlier in the campaign.

Arteta said of Madueke: “That’s the second part of the story – the first is that you need to earn the right to be able to do that.

“Noni did that, he reacted to not playing often, went away, started to train and was a different Noni.

“He wasn’t going to accept that situation, he meant business, and since then he has been incredible.

“Now you are in that position, he needs to hone and improve it, so let’s see if he can do that.”

Madueke was Arsenal’s most dangerous attacker against Hull, registering five successful dribbles and creating several opportunities.

Sky Sports also reports that he is now averaging 3.57 chances created per 90 minutes, the highest rate of any Premier League player this season.

Madueke’s improvement could be particularly important while Bukayo Saka is unavailable.

Earlier in the season, a January exit was even being discussed. CaughtOffside reported in September that Atletico Madrid and Juventus were monitoring his situation after his limited Premier League involvement.

That possibility now feels considerably less likely.

Madueke has responded exactly how Arteta would have wanted: rather than becoming frustrated, he worked independently during the international break and returned sharper. His passing accuracy, crossing and dribble success have all improved compared with last season.

There is still one obvious area to improve. Madueke had chances to score against Hull and could have made the victory far more comfortable.

But Arsenal now have a winger who is creating chances at an elite rate and increasingly influencing important matches. If he can add more ruthless finishing, Arteta may soon face a genuine selection dilemma when Saka returns.

3 Millionaire-Maker Quantum Computing Stocks

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The quantum computing market is full of exciting investments, but not all of them will pan out.

Quantum computing is an emerging industry that could have several huge winners in it. There are countless early-stage quantum computing stocks that I think could provide massive returns, as this technology is set to provide massive economic value once viability is reached. While that may seem like a far-fetched technology, the reality is many in the industry believe 2029 could be the year when viable quantum computing shows up.

That's right around the corner, so what stocks have huge potential? Let's take a look.

IonQ (IONQ +23.42%) is one of the first stocks investors think of when they hear about quantum computing. It was the first quantum computing stock to go public, and it has performed exceptionally well as a company, even if the stock is currently well off its all-time highs.

IonQ currently has one of the most accurate quantum computing technologies, with a 99.99% two-qubit gate fidelity score. IonQ has achieved this thanks to its trapped-ion approach to quantum computing, which yields excellent accuracy but at the cost of processing speed.

Next year, IonQ has plans to release its 256-qubit system, which could drive huge growth for the company as clients adopt early-stage quantum computing technology. That could be the event that is needed to turn IonQ's stock around and could kick-start huge returns.

Quantinuum (QNT +25.99%) is one of the newer quantum computing stocks on the public market. However, it's quickly emerging as a top option. Quantinuum just published results of 99.9995% logical fidelity, which isn't the same measure as IonQ's, but is still a very impressive result.

Quantinuum also uses trapped-ion technology like IonQ, which gives it an advantage in the accuracy measure of quantum computing. It believes it can achieve fault-tolerant quantum computing by 2029, and if it could do that, it would achieve impressive shareholder returns along the way.

Rigetti Computing (RGTI +19.29%) is taking a different approach than these two. It's focusing on superconducting computing, which hasn't yielded the greatest accuracy results. However, superconducting computing can operate at far greater speeds than trapped-ion computing, which will be a major advantage if Rigetti can get its accuracy where it needs to be.

Rigetti's two-qubit fidelity for its largest 108-qubit system is 99.1% (comparable to IonQ's figure) or 99.9% for a single qubit (comparable to Quantinuum's figure). As Rigetti's qubit size decreases, its accuracy goes up, but those systems are so small that doing anything useful on them isn't really viable.

Rigetti's goal is to produce a 1,000-qubit system with 99.9% two-qubit gate fidelity over the next three years, but that will be significantly smaller and less accurate than anything IonQ or Quantinuum is producing. However, because of Rigetti's speed advantage, it may be a longer-term quantum computing play that could make a comeback over the next few years.

We'll see how it pans out, but it's still a viable investment option.

One thing investors need to keep in mind with this trio is that there is no guarantee any of them will reach profitability. These are very high-risk stocks that could provide a high reward, but there's no guarantee either. There are countless impressive competitors in this space, many of whom have massive cash flows to fund quantum computing operations. These three are all upstarts and must continue to get funding from outside sources until they have a commercially viable product and business in place.

If they can make it to that stage, then they will be huge winners and a successful investment. But the reality is there will be a lot of failure in this industry too. Investors need to choose the best of the best, and these three represent that.

Keithen Drury has positions in IonQ. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends IonQ. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Octopus on the Camino Trail

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We both slept exceptionally well last night. With only a short 9-miles stretch to Melide ahead of us, we were no rush to hit the trail.

Our first order of business was finding a cafe for breakfast. When we arrived, the place was packed, but the crowd cleared out quickly. Before long, we had the place to ourselves. We sat back and watched through the window as hordes of walkers passed by on the street outside. We were more than happy to let them get ahead so we could enjoy a quiet, more peaceful hike.

After a wonderful breakfast of eggs, great bacon, and hot coffee, our tramily members Robert and Jill caught up with us. By 9:30 AM, we were all back on the trail together.

Shortly after starting, we spotted a large group of hikers boarding a tour bus. They were likely being shuttled to a spot further down the trail to collect another passport stamp before heading on to Santiago. While the popular trail moto is “Hike Your Own Hike” (HYOH), this felt more like bus your own hike (BYOH)!

It was a gorgeous morning featuring blue bird skies, warm sun, and a crisp, cool breeze. Leaving Palas de Rei, the Camino entered a beautiful patch of woods along an ancient road. As we emerged from the forest, rolling hills and towering mountains opened up in the distance. I certainly didn’t expect this much dramatic typography on this Camino.

Our next milestone was the tiny medieval hamlet of San Xulian where we stepped inside a charming chapel to collect our first stamp for the day.

Leaving the village, we stepped onto a deeply sunken path with 15-foot-high earthen banks on either side—the result of centuries of footsteps wearing down the soil. These ancient roads are easily some of my favorite sections of the entire trek.

The trail then guided us through a magnificent eucalyptus forest filled with massive, towering trees. As we exited the canopy, we ran right back into the weekend crowds. Having developed our solid “trail legs” over hundreds of miles, Knockerz and I were moving at a much faster pace than the casual walkers. Passing them proved tricky, however, as large groups frequently blocked the narrow paths without realizing it.

As we approached Melide, we noticed numerous tour buses parked along the road. Apparently, this was where the “BYOH” crowd rejoined the path after skipping the 9 miles of walking in between! Melide is also a major trail milestone: it’s the official junction where the Camino Primitivo merges into the Camino Francés.

We arrived at 1:00 PM to a town bustling with energy. Knowing that siesta was fast approaching and the streets would soon turn into a ghost town, we set out to grab lunch immediately.

Knockerz was craving a hamburger, though she was disappointed to find the beef patty was blended with pork. Meanwhile, I couldn’t pass up Melide’s most famous specialty: Pulpo a la Gallega (Galician-style octopus). Traditionally boiled in large copper kettles, sliced into thick pieces, seasoned with paprika, and served on round wooden plates, it was delicious! Knockerz got a bit squeamish watching me eat the tentacles with their large suckers attached, but it was a highlight for me.

After lunch, we searched for Melide’s famous local pastry—a traditional anise-flavored donut (melindres)—but siesta had already begun and the bakeries were locked up tight. Fortunately, we found a tiny grocery store that remained open and picked up a bottle of Crianza Rioja wine.

It turned out to be a perfect afternoon with warm sun and a cool, refreshing breeze. We spent the rest of the day relaxing on the top terrace of our albergue, sipping Rioja while soaking in sweeping views of the distant Galician hills.

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

After decades of no backpacking an abrupt life incident forced me back into the woods for some shinrin-yoku where I ran into a NOBO tramily at Shuckstack Fire Tower. That started an idea of a longer term forest bath on the AT. What a long strange trip its been. Any financial donations I get will be used to send out love to other thru hikers a trail magic.

Tesla Stock Hasn't Closed at a Record in Nearly 11 Months. Here's How Its Past Record Droughts Ended.

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The EV maker has been here six times before. What did shareholders get once each wait ended?

Tesla (TSLA +4.23%) last closed at a record on Dec. 16, 2025, at $489.88 a share. That was 327 days ago, or nearly 11 months. With Tesla shares trading near $346 as I write this, the stock would have to gain about 41% just to tie it.

Long gaps between records are nothing new for the EV maker, though. Since its IPO in June 2010, Tesla stock has gone 300 days or more without a record close six other times, based on daily split-adjusted closing prices.

Every one of those droughts eventually ended. But what happened next was very different. Two of them gave way to rallies of over 600%. The other four were followed by a new drought within months, and twice within days.

And the difference between the two groups probably boils down to whether Tesla's profits were turning higher.

The first two droughts came early in the electric-vehicle maker's life as a public company. The stock spent 474 days without a record from Nov. 24, 2010, to March 12, 2012, and then exactly 300 days from April 3, 2012, to Jan. 28, 2013. The next two lasted longer, at 942 days from Sept. 4, 2014, to April 3, 2017, and 821 days from Sept. 18, 2017, to Dec. 18, 2019. Most recently, shares went 1,133 days from Nov. 4, 2021, to Dec. 11, 2024, and then 364 days from Dec. 17, 2024, to last December's record.

That makes the current 327-day wait the second shortest of the seven. The middle of the six past droughts is around 650 days, or almost twice as long as this one has lasted.

Of course, the drops along the way were steep. In the 2021-to-2024 drought, the stock's lowest close was around 74% below its prior record.

And in every one of the six, shares closed at least 31% under the old high at some point. This time, the low so far was $298.32 on July 29, around 39% below the December record.

Two of them ended in huge runs. Once the 300-day gap closed on Jan. 28, 2013, Tesla stock kept setting records until Sept. 4, 2014, rising around 652% in that time.

And once the drought ended on Dec. 18, 2019, shares climbed about 1,464% to the November 2021 record that started the longest wait of all.

The other four runs were short. The March 2012 record led to just 22 days of new highs before the next drought began. The April 2017 record brought around five and a half months of records and a 29% climb.

The December 2024 run of records lasted six days. Last December's record ended one drought and began the current one the same day.

In other words, a new record on its own hasn't said much about what happens next. For shareholders, I'd say this matters more than the length of any drought.

What the two big runs share is a turn in profits.

In May 2013, Tesla said it had become profitable in the first quarter "for the first time in our ten year history," earning $11 million. The stock gained about 24% the day after the letter came out.

The 2019 run followed a similar swing. The company made $143 million in the third quarter of 2019. That was a sharp turn from losses of $702 million and $408 million earlier that year. Even more, its net income hit $721 million in 2020 -- after losing money in each of the four years before that.

But the four short runs had nothing like that behind them. The company lost $396 million in 2012 and almost $2 billion in 2017.

And its operating income (profit from the business before interest and taxes) was falling in both 2024 and 2025, down 20% and then another 38%.

That trend hasn't turned yet. In the first nine months of 2026, Tesla's operating income totaled about $2.1 billion, down about 30% from the same period in 2025.

What does this history suggest for anyone owning the stock now?

I don't think 11 months without a new high is a reason to sell on its own. After all, waits this long have been normal for the growth stock, and every one of them has ended.

But the next record might matter less than what's behind it. One that comes with operating income climbing again may look a lot more like 2013 or 2019. Without that, a new high might fade as fast as the last two did.

Daniel Sparks has clients with positions in Tesla. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Liverpool still remain undeterred. They believe Vuskovic is a huge talent and a defender ideally suited to Andoni Iraola’s style of play, but it should be said that reports elsewhere claim the likes of Real Madrid, Barcelona and Bayern Munich are watching the situation too.

Children were among the injured people seen being carried into a nearby hospital

Media linked to the Houthi movement in Yemen say at least 33 people, including women and children, have been killed in air strikes by Saudi Arabia.

There has been no comment from the Saudi-led coalition backing the internationally recognised government in Yemen.

Live video footage on Al Masirah - a television station run by the Houthis - has shown rescuers pulling bodies out of the rubble of several buildings in al-Shariyah, a town to the east of the capital, Sanaa.

The bodies of young children can be seen among those dug out from the ruins.

The television station has also shown a number of injured people being carried on stretchers to hospital.

Saba news agency, which is also run by the Houthis, said the attack was carried out by Saudi Arabia on Saturday. It added that 39 people had been injured.

The Saudis have led a coalition that has been fighting the Houthis for more than a decade in support of Yemen's internationally recognised government.

The war in Yemen had been dormant for several years but reignited as a result of the Iran war.

A truce began to fall apart when Saudi Arabia struck the airport in Sanaa - which is controlled by the Houthis - to prevent an Iranian plane landing there.

The Tehran-backed Houthis subsequently targeted Abha airport in Saudi Arabia, then began hitting Saudi ships that have been using the Red Sea off the coast of Yemen as an alternative oil export route to the Strait of Hormuz.

Major fighting has since resumed in Yemen between the Houthis and forces fighting for the Saudi-backed government of Yemen.

On Friday, an attack on a market in Taiz province, which has been the focus of much of the recent fighting, is reported to have killed 30 people.

The Houthis accused the Saudis of carrying out the strike, while government forces accused the Houthis of having fired a missile at the site.

The United Nations says more than 1,600 people have been killed and more than 300,000 displaced since fighting resumed.

The Saudis have regularly bombed Houthi positions, while the group has fired multiple missiles at targets inside Saudi Arabia, including airports and oil facilities.

Hours before the strike that hit al-Shariyah, the Saudi-led coalition said it intercepted a missile heading towards Riyadh.

A Houthi leader, Mahdi al-Mashat, has vowed that the group will take revenge for the latest attacks in Yemen.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Dropping Medicare Supplement - risks?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →


I'm sorry for being off-topic... I'm new to Medicare and would like to know why the Medigap premium is $600/month for your father and it's only $200/month for your mother? Are they not on the same plan? Is there a large age difference between the two? Thank you.
I don't know if they are on the same plan. What I can tell you:

-My mother could change plans as she has no pre-existing conditions while my father cannot due to having to go through underwriting.

-Male and female premiums can be different. Obviously if so, you wouldn't expect a 3x differential. Their ages are within 6 months of each other. She is likely on a different plan. I'll ask.

-Though premiums cannot be increased due to health/claims, there could be a death spiral type of situation. Meaning when my father got the plan there were X people his age in the pool. As the premiums went up over the last 19 years the healthier people could have dropped out (gone to Medicare Advantage or a different supplement). That could leave a bunch of people with a lot of expensive conditions/claims in the pool hence high rates.

I've seen this with other members of the family. In states where you have to undergo underwriting to change supplements (Medigap), premiums can skyrocket and you have little recourse if you don't want to go to Medicare Advantage. If you think about the risks of an 80 or 90 year old incurring big medical costs, I am not saying the premiums are totally unjustified.

FWIW, I am no expert but I have worked in the health insurance industry.


-
yes this is a concern. i believe it is called deadpooling.

U2 Weigh In on Ed Sheeran and Macklemore Tour Controversy

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Edge and Larry Mullen Jr. defended the rapper's right to speak out, while the Edge said Sheeran "should not be vilified".

U2 have weighed in on Macklemore’s removal from Ed Sheeran’s U.S. tour, backing the rapper’s right to speak out while cautioning against blaming Sheeran.

“We totally support Macklemore’s right as an artist to say what he wanted to say,” the Edge said in an interview with BBC News. “I don’t think Ed should be vilified,” he added, acknowledging the difficult position Sheeran had been in. The guitarist also praised the artists who left Sheeran’s tour in solidarity, framing the dispute as a matter of free expression. Related Deep Purple's Ian Gillan Shares Encouraging News After Undergoing Surgery Richard Lloyd, Television Guitarist, Dies at 75 Benson Boone Brings Young Fan Battling Cancer Onstage at Brisbane Concert Explore Ed Sheeran Macklemore U2 See latest videos, charts and news

Drummer Larry Mullen Jr. went further. “Artists can choose whether they want to be politically active or not. It’s a human right,” he said. “But we as a band will fight for the right to speak your mind as long as it doesn’t incite violence. And even if we disagree with it or don’t like it, nobody has a right – no billionaire, no promoter, no owner has the right to censor anybody. They will definitely not be censoring us ever.”

Macklemore was dropped from the North American leg of Sheeran’s Loop Tour in September after two opening sets at MetLife Stadium in New Jersey, where he said “Free Palestine,” spoke about Gaza and the West Bank, and performed his protest song “Hind’s Hall.” Promoter Messina Touring Group said venues on the remaining dates would not allow the shows to go ahead with him on the bill.

New England Patriots owner Robert Kraft confirmed he barred the rapper from Gillette Stadium, citing his comments and what Kraft called “a broader history of antisemitic rhetoric and imagery.”

Sheeran said the decision belonged to the promoter. Several acts left the tour in response, including Finneas, Lukas Graham and Irish folk band Beoga, which had been performing with Sheeran each night. Sheeran later opened a show in Philadelphia by apologizing for “mistakes” in his handling of the situation.

Macklemore has since played three sold-out Free Palestine benefit concerts in Dublin, Paris and London, with guests including Hozier, Kneecap and Beoga, raising money for organizations supporting Palestinians.

U2 release Carnaval De Luz, their 16th studio album and first full-length of new material in nine years, on Nov. 13. The album closes with “Torn,” a duet with the late Dolly Parton that the band says was her last recorded vocal performance.
