Cristian Mungiu’s Palme d’Or Winner ‘Fjord’ Takes Jakarta World Cinema Audience Award as Short Film Winners Land Debut Feature Funding

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Cristian Mungiu’s Palme d’Or winner “Fjord” took the audience award at Jakarta World Cinema, which wrapped with Banjong Pisanthanakun’s Thai horror film “Inherit” and handed three Indonesian short filmmakers funding for their first features.

“Fjord,” which also opened the festival, won the Jakarta World Cinema Audience Award, the event’s only feature prize.

The closing ceremony also unveiled the winners of the KlikFilm Short Movie Competition, run by the Indonesian streaming platform that powers the festival. Open for submissions from April to July, the competition drew more than 300 shorts from across Indonesia. The jury comprised horror director Joko Anwar (“Impetigore”), writer-director Gina S. Noer (“Two Blue Stripes”) and Khozy Rizal, whose short “Basri & Salma in a Never-Ending Comedy” played in competition at Cannes. Novo Nordisk Just Delivered Great News for Investors. Is a Comeback Finally Underway?

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Novo got a much-needed boost from its latest earnings report.

Are things finally starting to look up for Novo (NVO +0.03%)? The Denmark-based pharmaceutical leader has had a rough past couple of years, but its third-quarter financial results were strong enough to send the stock up 6% post-earnings. That's great news for shareholders, but can Novo maintain this momentum?

At first glance, Novo's third-quarter results didn’t seem particularly impressive, as some of the company’s longtime growth drivers underperformed. For instance, sales of Ozempic, the company's famous diabetes medicine, were 29.7 billion Danish kroner (about $4.5 billion), down 3% compared to the year-ago period. The company's weight-loss medicine, Wegovy, recorded sales of 20.4 billion DKK (about $3.1 billion), roughly flat compared with the prior-year quarter.

However, several other medicines helped pick up the slack, and arguably none was more important than Novo's oral Wegovy. The company launched this weight loss pill -- the first of its kind to earn approval -- in January. The Wegovy pill has helped attract many patients who were hesitant to start treatment with injectables. Novo reported that, as of June, oral Wegovy had surpassed three million prescriptions, and more than 80% were for patients who had never taken GLP-1s before. In the third quarter, oral Wegovy generated 5.4 billion DKK (about $810 million) in revenue.

Novo's total net sales were 79.2 billion DKK (about $11.9 billion), up 6% year over year. The company's net income dropped by 16% year over year to 16.7 billion DKK (about $2.5 billion), largely due to non-cash impairment charges. Still, Novo’s results beat analyst estimates, and the pharmaceutical company also raised its guidance for the fiscal year 2026.

It now expects revenue to be flat (at the midpoint) year over year, while the previous guidance implied a 3% year-over-year drop at the midpoint. It's no wonder Novo's shares jumped following this beat-and-raise quarter. But what's next for the drugmaker?

There are several catalysts ahead that could help Novo improve its financial performance. Let's consider three of them. First, the company could make meaningful progress with oral Wegovy. The medicine has now been approved in several other countries, including Germany and the U.K.

These markets might not be as lucrative as the U.S., but they should move the needle for the company. Novo will also likely attract brand-new GLP-1 patients in these regions. The company is also looking to earn label expansions for oral Wegovy. Novo has submitted an application to the U.S. Food and Drug Administration for oral Wegovy as a potential treatment for metabolic dysfunction-associated steatohepatitis.

The original injectable version of Wegovy is approved for this indication, but the pill offers a more convenient option. It could be successful in this niche and continue driving Novo's sales higher. Second, Novo will launch new products to strengthen its position in the diabetes and weight-loss markets.

It is inching closer to approval for CagriSema, a medicine that beat Wegovy in a head-to-head Phase 3 clinical trial for weight loss. CagriSema also posted strong clinical trial results in diabetes. This medicine could help Novo steal some market share from its biggest competitor, Eli Lilly (LLY -0.79%).

Third, Novo has several pipeline candidates that could improve its approved lineup over time. The company's Phase 3 pipeline features zenagamtide, a promising diabetes and weight-loss medicine that is being developed in subcutaneous and oral formulations.

The weight loss market is growing rapidly, and this product, along with others, may help Novo ride that tailwind for a long time, even as competition intensifies. Novo may still experience some clinical setbacks, but after the beating it took over the past two years, the company is slowly staging a comeback. In my view, the stock is attractive following its third-quarter results.

Prosper Junior Bakiny has positions in Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Over Pinchot Pass — John Muir Trail Day 10

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The night before, at Rae Lake, I ate some kind of buffalo stew, which was supposed to provide about 900 calories.  It is funny how flavor (usually) takes a backseat to caloric value on the trail.  I am just so conscious that I am exerting myself — a lot — and need to maximize my fuel intake.

After dinner I had my headlamp on in the tent, probably dictating my notes, and was not aware that Evelyn (or Serena?) was taking pictures of my tent in the darkness under a starry sky.

Night camping at Rae Lakes (photo courtesy of Evelyn or Serena)

My sleep started deep and sound.  Then I did wake up, coughing a bit, and I thought, “Oh, no!”  But it was still much less than before, and I was very tired, so I slept again.  I vaguely remember dreaming about how some people were organizing something-or-other and I was confused how it was going to work.  Maybe it meant I was wondering how to manage this journey?

Around 4:40 in the morning, when I got up to pee, I checked the Garmin and saw the time.  I thought, “Well, I think I’m not going to go back to sleep.”  So I didn’t; instead I fetched my food from the bear box, and ate my breakfast.  But I felt sluggish, like I was swimming through molasses.

How sluggish?  Well, I rose at 4:40 but I did not get on the trail until 7:00 a.m.  Despite everything, it was only thirty minutes later than the previous day, but it was far from efficient.  I said goodbye to Evelyn and Serena; they were heading southbound, and I was heading northbound.  I left the crowded campsite and started on my way.

One consequence of the Rae Lake crowds is that it was hard to find a private place to dig a cathole.  I decided to wait for a better place.  About a half hour down the trail, I pulled off and found a spot that was quite lovely and dug my hole and did my business.  In the middle of it, a deer appeared in the meadow next to me and gazed at me curiously.

Cat hole in the foreground, amazing view in the background!

Less than a half mile further, I discovered a beautiful camping site with a bear box and no crowds at all.  I thought, “Oh my gosh, if I’d just known and been willing to take the risk to go a little further yesterday, it would have been a much nicer situation.”  It was too late for me, but I resolved to tell any southbound hikers I encountered that they would be more comfortable stopping at the last camp site before the main Rae Lake location.

More beautiful and fewer crowds — the place to be!

As I looked at the long day ahead of me, I realized it would be 6-7 miles, all downhill, followed by 8-9 miles all uphill, followed by a mile or two of downhill to my next camp site at Lake Marjorie.  In elevation I started at 10,500 feet, would drop to 8,500 feet, and then would cross Pinchot Pass at 12,200 feet.  I planned for a long day.

I did about four miles on that nice downhill to start the day when I encountered a small stream I had to cross.  In general, these stream crossings had not been too challenging, frankly.  I brought my water shoes explicitly because I thought I would wade through river crossings, perhaps as much a thigh-deep, and there hadn’t been any like that – at least so far.

This one was no different, a two-stone hop across with a couple of different options as to route.  That, it turned out, was the problem.  As I prepared to take my second step I decided to go via a different sequence of stones, and I turned slightly to change direction.  It was a slight turn, nothing notable about it at all.  Except… Suddenly I felt a sharp pain in my left knee.

I let out a yowl and finished crossing.  But I was worried.  Clearly, I had injured something. The only question was, what was it and how badly was it damaged?

I was still able to move it, so that was a good first sign.  Working it gently, it barked at me in certain angles; but just going straight ahead, it seemed not to bother me too much.

I decided to take an Ibuprofen at the bottom of the hill to prevent swelling from whatever it was that had happened.  Five minutes later I changed my mind, thinking, “Why am I waiting  to take the medication?  I just sustained the injury.  I should take the ibuprofen immediately, right NOW.”  And I did.

I continued down gingerly, favoring my knee a bit, worried that this might be something serious, and not knowing whether it would be or not.

My main distraction was lizards.  Yes!  I had observed no reptiles at all above 10,000 feet, but coming lower they started to appear.  I think they were most likely Western Fence Lizards, but they could also have been Sagebrush Lizards.  Because of my focus on my knee, I did not photograph them, but I resolved to do so the next time they appeared.

Finally, I reached the bottom of the downhill.  There was a suspension bridge to cross there, and it was one of the bouncier ones I had ever been on.  With a GoPro I would gladly have filmed myself crossing it, but with my hand-held phone it was too easy to imagine it flying out of my hand into the rushing waters below.

From solid ground I took a couple of pictures of the bridge anyway.

Then I crossed to the other side and began the big uphill.  I was facing around 3,600 feet of elevation gain, while worrying about my stupid knee.  I also worried that the trail might feel kind of endless, and it did feel kind of endless.

Along the way I started leapfrogging another hiker named Amy.  Our hiking styles almost made it inevitable.  I would go fast but then stop and rest; she kept a steady pace.  So, I would go by her moving faster, and she would move by me when I stopped.  Then I would resume and pass her again, and the cycle would continue.  At one point, when I was ahead, I saw a grouse.  I wondered if it would still be there when she came by.

The good news in all of this was that either the Ibuprofen was working, or my knee was not badly injured, or both.

Amy and I had both set Lake Marjorie, on the other side of Pinchot Pass, as our destination for the night.  This was the first camping space about a mile and a half below the summit.

We kept ascending.  The one advantage was that the ascent took place over many miles, so it was rarely going up very steeply.  But over the course of the afternoon, it was feeling quite relentless.  Only the series of waterfalls along the way kept me engaged and appreciative.

…another waterfall falling in perpetuity!

Approaching Pinchot Pass I ran into a guy coming downhill toward me.  We chatted and I learned that, like me, he was 69 years old.  He seemed very demoralized.  He said he felt like he was shuffling, like he could barely even do it.  He said he was planning to get off at Kearsarge Pass and probably would not return.  I said, “Well, what you should do is get off at Kearsarge and go to Independence and then spend a day there doing nothing.  After a day off trail, your mind will be clearer, and then you can decide if that’s the end for you, or if you do actually want to come back.”  That was my recommendation; I don’t know if he took it.

Nearing the top, there were many rocks patterned with white striations.  I wanted to turn to Sam and ask the geologist to explain what had caused them.  But of course, he was back in Boulder by then.

You can see the white stripes in the foreground rocks, but also in the background cliff face. Quartz maybe? Sam could have told me…

The end of the trail up to the pass was a series of switchbacks.  You couldn’t really see where they were leading until, suddenly, ta-da!, you had reached the saddle of the pass.

It was a really, really hard day.  I started at seven a.m. and I got to the pass at exactly five p.m.  If you do the math, that’s ten hours.  And I still had the mile and a half to go down to the lake.

The views over the lakes as the sun was setting were beautiful.  Amy finally caught up to me again, at a stream where I stopped to top off my water supply before making camp.  That late in the afternoon, we were both worried that the camping spots might already be filled up.  But when I saw a truly beautiful location, on nice flat sandy ground, I said, “That’s the one for me!”  Amy kept going to the next spot further down the trail, but I called it a day.

Arriving at Rae Lake the previous day, I was so tired it was difficult to function.  On this occasion, at Lake Marjorie, I set up calmly and reasonably efficiently, and it just felt much more natural than it had up to that point.  I pitched my tent, soaked my food, and set up my sleeping system, of course.  But I also went down to the lake shore where I washed my socks and washed my feet.  While I had my feet in the lake’s icy cold water, I thought, “Oh, I need to ice my knee too.”  So, I went a little deeper into the lake and got my knee in the icy cold water as well.

Unfortunately, my dinner was disgusting.  Some of these freeze-dried meals work great for a cold-soaker, but some really need boiling water to be edible.  I just wish I had kept track of which was which for future hikes!  Dinner was the low point of the day (other than my knee tweak), but I ate it for the calories.

Early Evening by Lake Marjorie. Notice my tent to the right of the boulder and — at that point — nothing to the left of the boulder.

Sitting in my tent pitched next to a large boulder it was getting colder-and-colder at our high elevation.  It was definitely a night for my sleeping bag liner!  In deep twilight I heard a large animal moving around on the other side of the big boulder.  Initially I was quite spooked, but when I checked I saw that a late-arriving hiker was setting up camp there.

For a moment I was annoyed that my delicious privacy had been disturbed.  Then I overcame that feeling and went over to welcome the newcomer and see if they needed anything.  It was a solo hiker named Sally, born in China but now an Australian citizen, also NOBO on the John Muir Trail.  She was friendly and we chatted for a few moments before I returned to my tent to call it a night.

It had been a 17-mile day, my longest yet on the trail.  Sleep came easily.

In my 69 years I’ve done a lot of cool things – I’ve crossed the Atlantic in a 40-foot sailboat, bushwacked 100 miles across the north slope of the Alaska Range, hiked for 26 days in Western Nepal crossing five passes over 5,000 meters, and climbed Mt. Kilimanjaro, to name a few. It was my honor to serve my country as a diplomat around the world for 34 years. I have been active in retirement, and I hiked the Wonderland Trail in 2024 and the Long Trail in 2025. This year I'm trying... the John Muir Trail!

Modern life is changing the DNA of bacteria in our guts

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Gut bacteria can acquire genes from neighboring microbes, adding to the genetic differences linked to how people live.

Diet, antibiotics, and indoor plumbing help shape which bacteria settle into a person’s gut. That much has been clear for years.

What researchers couldn’t say is whether the bacteria themselves have been evolving in response.

A study of nearly 6,000 gut bacterial genomes finds that they have.

Across the species examined, strains from industrialized communities carried more genes than those from non-industrialized communities.

Most of the extra genes arrived recently. The bacteria didn’t inherit them from a parent cell. They took them from other microbes living alongside them.

So modern life hasn’t only changed which bacteria live in the gut. The same species now carries a different set of genes depending on how its host lives.

Researchers at Kiel University’s Institute of Clinical Molecular Biology and MIT’s Department of Biological Engineering ran the work, with the evolutionary modeling done in Japan.

Everything here was measured in bacterial genomes, so none of it shows that a bigger bacterial genome makes a person healthier or sicker.

All of the genomes come from the Global Microbiome Conservancy, a non-profit effort to bank living strains from communities that microbiome research has mostly passed over.

For this study, 20 volunteers in Cameroon, Ghana, Malaysia, Nigeria and Rwanda gave fresh stool samples. None had taken antibiotics recently.

Scientists processed each sample on site within three hours. The samples were then frozen in liquid nitrogen at minus 321°F (minus 196°C).

In the lab, the material went onto plates inside an oxygen-free chamber. Colonies grew at 99°F (37°C) for one to two weeks. Single colonies were then picked and sequenced.

The cultures yielded 1,841 new bacterial genomes. Added to 4,140 strains already banked, the collection reached 5,981 genomes. They came from 56 donors in nine countries.

Researchers classified donors as industrialized or non-industrialized using a development score for where they lived, compared with their country’s median.

From that collection, the team picked ten species sampled well on both sides of the divide, with at least eight strains from each side. Most are Bacteroides and close relatives, the workhorses of the lower gut.

E. coli was left out on purpose. Its genetic diversity has already been mapped in detail.

In all ten species, strains from industrialized guts carried more protein-coding genes. The difference was statistically significant in eight of the ten species.

Core genes, the ones every strain of a species has, showed no difference. The extra material was all optional genes, the kind one strain carries and the next one doesn’t.

The optional sets also varied more from strain to strain in industrialized hosts, again in all ten species. The team then widened the comparison to 34 species and saw the same pattern.

A bigger genome could mean two different things. Either these strains picked up genes lately, or strains that already had big genomes happened to do well in modern guts.

Separating the two fell to a group in Okinawa, Japan. They compared the family tree of a single gene with the family tree of the species carrying it.

A mismatch between the two marks the spot where a gene was gained, lost or handed over.

Gene gains clustered near the tips of the evolutionary trees, indicating that they occurred relatively recently.

They also piled up on the branches belonging to industrialized hosts.

Between 80% and 90% of those gains were transfers from other microbes. Only a small share came down from a parent cell.

Many arrived packaged inside plasmids and viruses that infect bacteria.

Both deliver several genes at once. Swapping of this kind goes on well beyond the gut, in soil, seawater and thawing permafrost.

Gergely Szöllősi heads the Model-Based Evolutionary Genomics Unit at the Okinawa Institute of Science and Technology.

His group built these tools for a much older problem, and Lénárd Szánthó, a senior bioinformatician in the unit, adapted them to a far shorter stretch of time.

“These methods were built to look back hundreds of millions and billions of years, so it was not obvious that they would tell us anything about something this recent,” said Szöllősi.

“Lénárd made them work for this problem, and the picture that comes out is that bacteria in industrialized hosts have been acquiring genes from their neighbors recently, and this is what makes their genomes larger.”

About 5% of the gene families in a typical species collection tracked with the host’s way of life.

The team checked that against bacterial DNA read straight from the stool of 1,015 people, and 83% of the signals pointed the same way.

A few gene families turned up in more than one species, which is harder to put down to chance.

Two of them help build the outer coat of the cell, the part that other microbes and the immune system meet.

Another repeat hit was an enzyme that breaks down penicillin-type drugs. Genes like that ride the same mobile DNA now spreading antibiotic resistance through cities and farms.

The team also counted genes where protein-changing mutations had built up faster than mutations that did not change the protein. That pattern is the usual sign of a change being favored.

About 1.4% of the genes examined qualified. One of them helps a cell hold its outer envelope together under stress, and it carried the signature in three separate species.

In the five species with the most genomes, the researchers tracked single-letter DNA differences in core genes.

The variants tied to industrialized hosts kept landing on the same few jobs: building the cell wall, handling stress and making vitamin B12.

A companion study from the same group found more vitamin B12 production in the gut communities of people more exposed to industrialized environments.

Another cluster of changes hit the genes that build a molecule on the bacterial surface. The human immune system reads that molecule and reacts to it.

Researchers have not tested whether any of this changes how a person’s immune system behaves. The authors wrote that it is a possibility worth following up.

“I hope our results will inspire further studies to use this knowledge in supporting us to live a healthier life in our changing environment,” Szánthó said.

The analysis can’t say which part of industrialized living is responsible. Diet, medicine, sanitation and exposure to outdoor microbes all travel together, and this data can’t pull them apart.

Bigger genomes may not be an adaptation at all.

Extra genes build up on their own when the pressure to discard useless DNA eases, and the authors couldn’t rule that out.

Ten species is a thin slice of a gut that holds hundreds, and most of the ten belong to one bacterial group.

Species that have never been grown in a lab are missing here, which matters because industrialized guts hold fewer bacterial species to begin with.

The study itself doesn’t test what those extra genes actually do inside a person. Getting that answer will take experiments rather than more genomes.

The authors call for controlled experiments with bacteria, laboratory studies of evolution, and more detailed measurements of people’s living conditions.

The full study was published in the journal Cell.

MSG Says Macklemore Is “Not An Arena Level Act” After Turning Down Free Palestine Tour

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After Macklemore called out some of the venues that would not host his Free Palestine Tour, Madison Square Garden has responded.

In a recent statement, an MSG spokesperson claimed the decision to reject the tour was due to “extremely limited availability” and the fact that he’s “not an arena level act,” despite quickly selling out the tour’s arena shows in Dublin, Paris and London last month.

“We have some of the busiest venues in the world with extremely limited availability and he’s not an arena level act,” the MSG rep said in a statement.

The statement came after Macklemore called out some of the venues that “have turned us down” including MSG, Prudential Center, Barclays Center, UBS Arena, Intuit Dome, Kia Forum, Crypto.com Arena, Chase Center, and Climate Pledge Arena. Watch on Deadline

“By mid-September, we received available dates at these arenas through the end of the year. But once they found out these were my benefit shows for Palestine, those dates were suddenly off the table,” he said. “The doors were closed. The conversations were all different, but the result was the same. The shows were not going to be allowed to happen in these venues.”

Macklemore added in part, “I’d rather lose every opportunity in this industry than spend another day protecting people who value their business relationships with Israel more than they value Palestinian human life. We live in a country that prides itself on freedom of speech. Yet we’ve reached a point where a handful of billionaires can decide which causes are acceptable, which voices are welcome and which human lives are worth defending. Today it’s Palestine. What will it be tomorrow?”

In September, Macklemore announced the Free Palestine Tour, which is “supporting the Palestinian people and movement,” after Ed Sheeran dropped Macklemore from his tour amid an outcry around the opener’s “Free Palestine” onstage remarks. Opting to remain publicly neutral on the issue, Sheeran subsequently released a statement saying it was the “promoter’s decision” after Gillette Stadium owner Robert Kraft assembled several of the tour venues to give him an ultimatum.

5 Airbus A321neo Routes With Lie-Flat Business Class Seats

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Brandon's passion for aviation started at a young age. He became involved in the flight simulator and VATSIM communities, which sparked his interest in aviation and eventually led him to earn his private pilot license. Outside of work, he continues to build flight hours, explore new airports, and stay involved in the aviation community.

Lie-flat business class belonged to widebodies until June 15, 2014, when JetBlue Airways launched Mint on a single route between John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) and Los Angeles International Airport(LAX). The aircraft was an Airbus A321, a narrowbody that American, Delta, and United flew transcontinental routes with recliner seats at premium fares. JetBlue put flat beds in the front of one and changed what passengers expected from a single-aisle aircraft.

A decade later, the A321neo and its long-range derivatives carry lie-flat seats across both US transcontinental routes and the Atlantic. The aircraft's range made that possible. An A321LR can reach London from New York, meaning a narrowbody now competes directly with widebodies on routes that were closed to it a generation ago. The seats themselves have kept pace, with JetBlue fitting the Thompson Aero VantageSOLO in private suites with closing doors and direct aisle access on every Mint seat aboard its A321neo and A321LR fleets.

The operators are an unusual group. A US low-cost carrier flies the most A321LR transatlantic routes of any airline. An all-business-class boutique operates the type with 76 flat seats and nothing behind them. Two European flag carriers use it to open thinner North American routes that cannot support a widebody.

JetBlue launched Mint on June 15, 2014, on the route between New York John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) and Los Angeles International Airport (LAX). The transcontinental market was the right place to start because American, Delta, and United charged premium fares for recliner seats on the same city pair. JetBlue undercut them with flat beds and a markedly better onboard product, and the competitive response reshaped premium transcon flying across all four carriers.

The current product on the A321neo is a considerable step beyond what launched in 2014. The aircraft carries 16 Mint seats, comprising 14 Mint Suites and two Mint Studios, arranged 1-1, so every passenger has direct aisle access. JetBlue states that every Mint seat on its A321neo with Mint is a private suite with a door, and that all of them convert to a fully lie-flat bed with Tuft & Needle adaptive foam cushions and a T&N-designed pillow and comforter.

The two Mint Studios at the front stand out. JetBlue describes the Studio as carrying the largest TV on a US airline at 22 inches, plus a mirrored vanity, extra storage, and a guest seat and table that lets a second Mint passenger visit. The Points Guy measured the Studio bed at just over 82 inches in lie-flat mode, which it called the largest lie-flat bed on any US airline, and paid $199 above the standard Mint fare to book one on the inaugural. Aircraft type matters when booking. JetBlue's older A321 Classic with Mint also carries 16 Mint seats, but only four of them are suites with doors, with the remainder in alternating 2-2 rows.

The Airbus A321LR is a long-range derivative of the A321neo, which allows JetBlue to fly a narrowbody across the Atlantic. The aircraft carries 24 Mint seats, comprising 22 Mint Suites and two Mint Studios, arranged in the same 1-1 layout as the domestic A321neo. The seat is identical. JetBlue fits the Thompson Aero VantageSOLO across both fleets, which means a passenger flying JFK to Los Angeles and a passenger flying JFK to London Heathrow (LHR) are in the same product.

JetBlue serves Heathrow twice daily from JFK for the winter 2026-27 season, alongside JFK to Paris and Boston Logan International Airport (BOS) to both Heathrow and Paris. The wider European network reaches Amsterdam, Dublin and Edinburgh from New York and Boston, with Madrid and Milan added from Boston. That is more A321LR transatlantic routes than any other airline operates, a notable position for a carrier that flew nothing across the Atlantic before 2021.

The aircraft changes which routes are viable. A widebody crossing the Atlantic needs enough demand to fill 250 or more seats. An A321LR carries 138 in JetBlue's configuration, 24 of them Mint, which lowers the threshold considerably. That is why the type has opened city pairs between secondary European cities and the US East Coast that no airline served nonstop before. For passengers, it also means the business class cabin on a transatlantic flight may hold 24 seats rather than 60, which changes availability and award space in ways that cut both directions.

What's it like onboard American Airlines' Airbus A321XLR?

La Compagnie operates an Airbus A321neo with 76 lie-flat business class seats and no other cabin. The airline flies between Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR) and Paris Orly (ORY) as its core route, with the seats arranged 2-2 rather than the 1-1 layout JetBlue uses. That configuration means not every passenger has direct aisle access, which is the trade-off for fitting 76 flat beds into a narrowbody.

The aircraft replaced Boeing 757s in 2019, and the upgrade was substantial. The 757s carried angled-flat seats that recline to a sloped position rather than a true horizontal one, and the A321neo brought fully flat beds to a carrier whose entire proposition rests on the business class product. For an all-business-class airline, the seat is not one cabin among several. It is the whole aircraft and the whole business.

The economics are what make the route interesting. Simple Flying has described La Compagnie as offering one of the most consistently affordable ways to fly business class across the Atlantic, which works because the airline carries no economy cabin to subsidize and no connecting network to feed. Every seat sold is a business class fare, and the base cost of a single narrowbody is far lower than a widebody running three or four cabins. La Compagnie also serves Milan from Newark and adds Nice seasonally, applying the same model to leisure-weighted markets where premium demand concentrates in specific months.

TAP Air Portugal deploys its Airbus A321LRs primarily on routes between Lisbon Humberto Delgado Airport (LIS) and North America, serving destinations in both the United States and Canada. The business class seat is the Thompson Vantage, a lie-flat product that differs from the VantageSOLO JetBlue fits in that it is arranged in a staggered configuration rather than a 1-1 layout with every seat against a window.

The aircraft suits what TAP is doing geographically. Lisbon sits further west than any other major European hub, which shortens the Atlantic crossing and places North American cities within comfortable A321LR range. That advantage lets TAP serve secondary North American markets that would not support a widebody from a more easterly European base, and it underpins the airline's strategy of positioning Lisbon as a transfer point between the Americas and Europe.

TAP also uses the type beyond the Atlantic. The A321LRs operate to destinations in Africa and Brazil, markets where Portugal's historical links generate demand that is real but frequently too thin for a widebody year-round. A single aircraft type covering North America, Africa and South America from one hub gives TAP flexibility that a larger aircraft would not, and it means the same lie-flat business class cabin appears across a network spanning three continents. For passengers, the practical point is that a TAP long-haul flight may be operated by a narrowbody with a business cabin considerably smaller than the A330neos the airline flies on its denser routes.

The planemaker has big plans for the reimagining of business class travel.

Scandinavian Airlines (SAS) operates three Airbus A321LRs, fitting the Thompson Vantage in business class, and uses them on routes between Scandinavia and North America. It is the smallest fleet on this list by a wide margin, and that scarcity defines the operation. Three aircraft cannot cover a network, which means the type appears on specific routes at specific times rather than across the airline's transatlantic schedule.

The rationale matches TAP's. Scandinavian cities generate genuine demand for North American service, but frequently not enough to fill a widebody year-round on every city pair. An A321LR lowers the threshold for a nonstop to become viable, allowing SAS to serve markets that would otherwise require a connection through a larger European hub. The lie-flat business cabin makes those routes sellable to premium passengers, who determine whether a long-haul route works financially.

The fleet size also reflects SAS's commercial position. The airline entered Chapter 11 in 2024, moved from Star Alliance to SkyTeam in September 2024, and now has Air France-KLM as a shareholder. Three A321LRs are a cautious commitment rather than a strategic bet on the type, and the aircraft function as a tool for specific routes rather than a fleet strategy. For a passenger, the result is that booking SAS to North America may mean a widebody with a large business cabin or a narrowbody with a small one, depending entirely on the route and the day.

Here's What the Average Social Security Benefit Actually Covers

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The average Social Security check covers only a fraction of what seniors actually spend each year.

Most retirees lean on Social Security for much of their income, yet the average check was built to replace only part of a paycheck. As of September 2026, the average retired-worker benefit is $2,088.79 per month, according to the Social Security Administration (SSA) Monthly Statistical Snapshot. That adds up to $25,065.48 per year.

A household headed by someone 65 or older spends $61,432 per year, based on the BLS Consumer Expenditure Survey from 2024, the most recent data available. While housing and healthcare are major concerns, transportation and food costs also represent significant portions of a typical senior's budget.

The average annual benefit of $25,065.48 covers only 41% of that annual senior-household spending. This is by design, as the government built the program to replace roughly 40% of pre-retirement income. It's not intended to fund a complete lifestyle on its own.

The SSA calculates your benefit based on your highest 35 years of earnings, where earlier wages are adjusted for inflation, through the primary insurance amount (PIA) formula. This leaves an annual shortfall of $36,366.52 that you'll need to cover through other sources.

For retirees without a pension or deep savings, part-time work may be the only realistic way to fill that hole. Inflation adjustments provide some relief, but they're meant to maintain current buying power rather than increase it.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027, adding about $75 a month to that average check. This adjustment is designed to help your benefits keep pace with rising prices. The average check is a foundation, not a full budget, and the question is how to cover the rest.

It's possible to beat the average Social Security check by delaying your Social Security application. Every month you wait to apply increases your monthly benefits until you qualify for your largest checks at 70.

If you expect to have an average or above-average life expectancy and you can afford to cover your living costs another way in the meantime, delaying your Social Security application is a viable way to grow your checks. It could also lead to a much larger lifetime benefit.

Things like remaining in the workforce for longer or increasing your income today could also lead to larger Social Security checks in the future because the SSA bases your benefit on your average monthly income over your 35 highest-earning years. If you're earning more now than in years past, working a bit longer could boost your future benefits.

But even in the best-case scenario, your checks likely won't be enough to cover all your living expenses in retirement. Make sure you have a plan for how you'll cover what Social Security doesn't before you sign up.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

Popular Dairy Alternatives Could Be Leaving You Short of Essential Nutrients

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Replacing even a modest amount of dairy with plant-based alternatives could significantly reduce essential nutrient intake, according to a new study from the University of Surrey. Researchers found that substituting just 20% to 35% of dairy products increased the estimated prevalence of inadequate iodine intake among adolescent girls from 54% to 65%. Among women ages 19 to 49, the figure rose from 32% to 42%.

The study also identified reductions in calcium, riboflavin, and vitamin B12 intake. However, researchers found that fortifying plant-based alternatives and other foods could help prevent these nutritional shortfalls.

In the first UK study of its kind, researchers from the University of Surrey and Queen’s University Belfast analyzed dietary data from more than 3,500 people, ages 18 months to 100 years. Using the National Diet and Nutrition Survey, they modeled how replacing milk, yogurt, and cheese with commercially available plant-based alternatives would affect intake of four essential micronutrients.

Dr Katie Nicol, a research fellow at the University of Surrey and co-author of the study, explained:

“With the growing need to address environmental sustainability, many dietary recommendations are focused on plant-based diets and the reduced intake of animal products.”

“With the rise in plant-based alternative foods, we need to know whether they are like-for-like replacements for the micronutrients that are found in dairy products. This is particularly important for vulnerable groups, including young children and adolescent females, who are more likely to be deficient in certain micronutrients, which could affect their overall health.”

Iodine was the nutrient most affected by dairy replacement. When researchers modeled completely replacing dairy with plant-based alternatives under current fortification levels, the estimated prevalence of inadequate iodine intake reached 80% among adolescent girls and 72% among adolescent boys. These figures reflect modeled dietary inadequacy, not confirmed clinical deficiencies.

Iodine is essential for producing thyroid hormones, which regulate metabolism and support growth and brain development. It is particularly important before and during pregnancy, when iodine requirements increase.

Dr Sarah Bath, associate professor at the University of Surrey and lead author, said:

“The UK is particularly vulnerable to iodine insufficiency due to a lack of an iodine fortification policy such as universal salt iodization, which many other countries have.”

“People may also be unaware of the importance of iodine, which is needed to make thyroid hormones to regulate our metabolism, support healthy growth, and brain development. Iodine is particularly important during pregnancy, when the requirement increases, but it is also essential prior to pregnancy to ensure that stores are optimized. People are also unaware that dairy products are a major source of iodine, particularly in the UK, and that plant-based alternatives, unless fortified, have a very low iodine content.”

The researchers found that mandatory fortification of plant-based dairy alternatives could prevent declines in micronutrient intake. However, even with fortified alternatives, an estimated 59% of adolescent girls would still have inadequate iodine intake. Adding iodine to bread reduced that figure to 37%, suggesting that combining both fortification strategies could be more effective.

Dr Nicol emphasized the importance of aligning nutrition policies with environmental goals:

“Nutrition policy must evolve in tandem with environmental targets; however, it is important that shifts towards more sustainable diets do not compromise on public health. Fortification of foods is one way that this could be achieved; however, government policy is needed to make this happen.”

Dr Anne Nugent, from Queen’s University Belfast, described how the team identified dairy-containing foods in the UK National Diet and Nutrition Survey and modeled replacing them with equal amounts of plant-based alternatives. The researchers accounted for differences in the nutritional content of available products and tested whether fortification could prevent deficiencies without causing excessive nutrient intake.

“During the analysis, we first identified all foods and recipes containing dairy produce (milk, cheese, and yogurt) in the national food survey for the UK, the National Diet and Nutrition Survey. Next, we conducted a series of replacement scenarios whereby we substituted the dairy present with an equal weight of plant-based dairy alternatives. As the nutrient composition of dairy alternatives available on our supermarket shelves can vary, we also applied a condition to make sure that every dairy alternative we had identified in our previous market survey had an equal chance of selection. The last step was to examine the impact on dietary intakes of key nutrients following either the reduction or removal of dairy from the diet, replacing it with the dairy alternatives as above, and asking whether a series of fortification options could mitigate against any shortfalls in intake while avoid situations of excess.”

Reference: “The impact of replacing dairy products with plant-based dairy alternatives on iodine and other micronutrients: a modelling study and evaluation of policy options to support population intake” by Katie Nicol, Anne P Nugent, Jayne V Woodside, Kathryn H Hart and Sarah C Bath, , The Lancet Planetary Health. DOI: 10.1016/j.lanplh.2026.101515

In-N-Out’s billionaire owner vows iconic chain ‘will always be’ in California and says leaving the state would be the ‘dumbest business decision ever’

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lynsi Snyder, president and owner of In-N-Out, moved her family to Tennessee recently but reaffirmed that the iconic burger chain will never abandon California.

In an interview with actor Terry Crews that In-N-Out posted online, she was asked how the company will retain its California DNA as it expands to more states around the country.

“This is where we started. It’s where we are now, and it’s where we will always be,” Snyder replied.

Her grandparents opened the first In-N-Out in Southern California in 1948, and the company has gradually expanded to other parts of the state—and to other parts of the country.

While far from being the biggest chain, it retains a cult following that has helped its expansion as people who moved out of California make pilgrimages when restaurants open in new states.

But Snyder, who grew up in Northern California, revealed last year that she and her family were moving to Tennessee, saying that raising kids was difficult in the state and that “doing business is not easy” there either.

That sparked fears In-N-Out might leave California, prompting the burger heiress—who is worth an estimated $7.3 billion—to clarify that her departure from the state doesn’t mean the company will too.

In a video posted to her Instagram page last year, Snyder told followers: “We’re not leaving California, or leaving our roots behind. Each one of our locations is here to stay.”

Still, there has been a corporate exodus from California to states like Texas, where Elon Musk has relocated the headquarters for Tesla and SpaceX.

In-N-Out has also clashed with state authorities, such as when it refused to comply with officials and request that customers must show proof of vaccination to make purchases during the pandemic.

And the company reluctantly raised prices after California imposed higher minimum wages for fast-food workers in 2024.

But during her latest interview, Snyder addressed the fears about In-N-Out, which maintains headquarters in California and Tennessee.

“There were rumors about ‘they’re gonna close all their California stores.’ I’m like what where did this even come from? That’s unimaginable. I can’t even imagine that — dumbest business decision ever,” she said.

Snyder added that In-N-Out continues to grow in California, with “lots of stores” slotted for the other cities in the state.

At the same time, the company has expanded to Nevada, Arizona, Utah, Texas, Oregon, Colorado, Idaho, Washington and Tennessee. Snyder suggested that Mississippi, Arkansas, and Oklahoma are under consideration, with New Mexico also in the works.

But she insisted the company is taking a slow and steady approach to growth that won’t compromise on the quality of its food her grandparents’ values, adding that In-N-Out won’t be able to accommodate every request to open a new location.

As for her own family’s relocation to Tennessee, Snyder described it as “kind of a God thing” and said she originally wanted to move to Colorado because she loves the mountains.

But her husband floated the idea of going to Tennessee instead. While they already had made plans, she recalled that “something just kind of clicked.”

“I felt it and then started looking at it, just praying, and I’m like ‘I think you’re right. I think that’s where we’re supposed to go,'” Snyder said.

Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing.

One of Jon Bernthal's Best Performances Takes Less Than 10 Episodes To Binge

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Editor's note: The below article includes discussion of suicide.

Jon Bernthal has worked steadily in Hollywood for decades now. His versatility means that he's taken on projects as diverse as The Walking Dead, American Gigolo, and His & Hers. He brought The Punisher to life in multiple projects, appeared on the big screen in this summer's massive hit, The Odyssey, and won over audiences with his Broadway debut in Dog Day Afternoon. But there's one more project that Bernthal absolutely shines in, and it only takes nine episodes to binge.

The Bear premiered on Hulu in 2022 and quickly earned dedicated fans and rave reviews. The comedy series (although some people are still arguing whether it should have been classified as a drama) tells the story of a complex chef named Carmen "Carmy" Berzatto (Jeremy Allen White). Carmy has been trying to make it as a professional chef in the fine dining world, but decides to return to his hometown of Chicago after the death of his older brother, Mikey (Bernthal).

Satisfy your appetite for more chaotic drama with these similar shows.

Carmy takes over the family business and, over five seasons, transforms it from the Original Beef sandwich shop into a fancy restaurant called The Bear. A heartwrenching yet beautiful portrayal of a dysfunctional family and the far-reaching effects of trauma and addiction, The Bear became a pop culture favorite as well as a critical darling, with 22 Emmy wins throughout its run.

Because Mikey has already died by suicide before the first episode takes place, The Bear introduces his character through flashbacks that emphasize him as a massive presence that casts a shadow over the actions of many characters. When Carmy's big brother is first introduced in Season 1's "Ceres," he seems joyful, boisterous, and funny, and his strong relationships with his siblings and his "cousin" Richie (Ebon Moss-Bachrach) are obvious. But the trauma behind Mikey and his addiction is much more prevalent in Season 2's "Fishes," which spotlights the many toxic qualities of the Berzatto family at Christmastime. Mikey is explosive and off-balance, evidence of the emerging mental health struggles that lead to his untimely death. Season 3 delivers a glimpse at Mikey's kinder, more charming facets when he hires Tina (Liza Colón-Zayas) to work at The Beef, while the "Gary" prequel episode released between Seasons 4 and 5 follows Richie and Mikey on a misguided errand. The alternating sides of his personality are evident — fun-loving and adventurous one minute, belligerent and angry the next.

Some actors require a lengthy amount of time to develop their characters, yet in a short amount of screen time, Bernthal forms a fully three-dimensional individual whom viewers innately understand. While Mikey is only meant to be a supporting presence, Bernthal's grounded, authentic performance highlights why so much of the story is centered around the aftermath of his character's death. In the hands of a lesser actor, Mikey would have been a footnote to the series, but thanks to Bernthal's portrayal, he becomes the heart of The Bear.

Bernthal won a much-deserved Emmy for his work in The Bear Season 2 and was later nominated two more times. Although he only appears about once per season, his episodes serve as proof of his immense talent. The Bear has come to a close, with the final season wrapping up this past June, but it's still worth a binge to catch every awe-inspiring minute of Bernthal's performance.

Western Midstream: 9% Yield, And The Best Is Yet To Come In 2027 (Rating Upgrade)

I have covered Western Midstream Partners LP (WES) before, where I explained the investment thesis in detail and why I considered it one of the most attractive buys within the midstream sector. Since

Giants reportedly interested in Bucs star as trade rumors heat up

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The possibility of the New York Giants pursuing Vita Vea is an interesting storyline for the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, especially if the team keeps sliding down the standings. With the trade deadline approaching, a 3-6 record could force Tampa Bay into difficult conversations about its direction and whether holding on to every veteran piece makes sense. Vea is not just another defensive lineman. He is one of Tampa Bay's most important defensive pieces, and his ability to occupy multiple blockers, collapse the pocket, and create opportunities for the players around him makes him hard to replace. Losing him would undoubtedly leave a significant hole in the middle of the defensive line. ESPN's Jeremy Fowler has linked Vea to the Giants, with New York looking for help along its defensive front after failing to land Jeffery Simmons. FOX Sports reporter Ralph Vacchiano has also said the Giants have a strong interest in Vea and could pursue him aggressively. The Buccaneers, however, are not currently expected to move him.

The Giants' interest is legitimate, too. New York has already made an aggressive push to upgrade its defensive front, including pursuing Jeffery Simmons before ultimately trading him to Chicago. With that opportunity gone, Vea has emerged as another potential target. Reports suggest the Giants could be willing to make a substantial offer to close the deal. But would the Buccaneers actually entertain it?

A deal would make sense from a business perspective. Vea is 31, carries a significant financial commitment, and could command valuable draft compensation from a Giants team trying to make a playoff push. However, Tampa Bay also recently gave him a one-year, $30 million extension, keeping him under contract through 2027. Vea has made it clear he wants to remain a Buccaneer, making a departure especially difficult. The football side of this conversation is also important. Trading Vea would leave the Buccaneers without their most imposing interior presence and could make an already inconsistent defense even more vulnerable. If Tampa Bay still believes it can compete in the NFC South, moving him would be difficult to justify.

This article originally appeared on Bucs Wire: Giants reportedly interested in Bucs star as trade rumors heat up

Odegaard winner spares officials from another VAR row

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

An ultimately routine Arsenal win but, even by the standards of VAR, an utterly bizarre refereeing controversy. Mikel Arteta’s instant simmering fury at being denied a first-half penalty may also have turned into something rather more explosive had Martin Odegaard not later sent the champions six points clear with a ninth win in 10 Premier League games.

The big talking point arose with inadvertent theatre from referee Farai Hallam when Kai Havertz, who was clearly in an offside position, was equally clearly pulled down by Hull City defender Nobel Mendy inside the penalty area. Which comes first? The intervention of VAR suggested the latter. But Hallam duly went to check the pitch-side monitor before then announcing the former.

“After review, there is a holding offence by Blue No 32 on Red No 29,” he declared. Cue cheers from the home Arsenal crown. “However…,” he went on. “Red No 29 is in an offside position, so the final decision will be a free-kick for offside to Hull City”.

#ARSHUL – 42' VAR recommended an on-field review for a potential penalty to Arsenal for a challenge by Mendy on Havertz - but determined that Havertz was in an offside position in the build-up. REF: "After review, there is a holding offence by Blue No.32 on Red No.29. However, Red No.29 is in an offside position, so the final decision will be a free-kick for offside to Hull City".

It all sounded so decisive that most inside the Emirates instinctively thought the outcome reasonable. But then came a closer look at the rules. And Law 11.2 certainly did seem to support Arteta’s protestations: “In situations where a player in an offside position is moving towards the ball with the intention of playing the ball and is fouled before playing or attempting to play the ball, or challenging an opponent for the ball, the foul is penalised as it has occurred before the offside offence.”

Arteta clearly saw no value post-match in aiming much criticism at Hallam following a fourth 1-0 win in 10 league games but did still emphasise his surprise at the decision. “What do you think?” he asked one reporter. “I thought offside, but the law says that doesn’t matter,” said the journalist. “So not offside then,” said Arteta, who stressed that he needs to understand “what is overruling what in the decision-making” after a Premier League statement simply said that Havertz was deemed offside “in the build-up.”

At the very best, it all leaves further confusion and again opens refereeing body Pro Ref to the charge of inconsistency. Fabian Schär, for example, was awarded a penalty for Newcastle against Bournemouth in 2024, despite being in an offside position when his shirt was grabbed by Adam Smith.

Conversely, Dango Ouattara had a penalty overturned for Brentford against Leeds last season after going down under pressure from Gabriel Gudmundsson before being deemed offside. The crucial point seems to be whether the player has actually challenged for the ball at the time of the infringement and, in this case, the answer was surely “no”.

Premier League title triumphs, however, are often forged on forgettable wins in frustrating circumstances and this was ultimately a classic of the genre. With Noni Madueke continuing to step up superbly in the absence of Bukayo Saka, Arsenal’s combination of attacking persistence and defensive resilience was ultimately rewarded by Odegaard’s scrappy 72nd-minute shot. For young players, it was also every bit as educational as the VAR controversy might be for emerging officials.

Madueke had shot low to force a save by Hull goalkeeper Konstantinos Tzolakis, but Odegaard was somehow still first to the rebound, despite being the wrong side of the penalty spot and five yards behind Hull defender John Egan when the ball was struck.

“The way he scores, not the most beautiful, but the most effective,” said Arteta, who said that his team had sometimes tried too hard for a “perfect goal” and should instead be happy with something “more pragmatic”.

It was a well-judged assessment as Arsenal head into the international break with creative deficiencies, but still almost flawless in the cold hard currency of results. Three wins in three days, including at Anfield and in Prague, also underlines a certain champions mentality.

“I need to praise the players,” said Arteta. “It’s been very very demanding; very short time to recover with all the travel. The mentality is incredible. It’s still going to be super difficult. We can improve in a number of areas.”

When it was then put to him that Arsenal have been offside 35 times this season – with Havertz responsible for 15 of those – Arteta acknowledged that “timing” was one element. But, with Havertz targeted by some frustrated fans, he stressed that responsibility was always shared for those split-second moments by the passer and receiver.

He also singled out Madueke, again easily Arsenal’s most creative player, following his surprise omission from Thomas Tuchel’s England squad. “He went away, started to train, he came back and it was a different Noni,” said Arteta, of his reaction to also being out of the Arsenal team earlier in the season. “You could sense that he meant business and he wasn’t going to accept the situation and, since then, I think he’s been incredible.”

An improved showing by Jaissle’s Newcastle, but they will be dissapointed that they failed to see it through after Jacob Murphy scored in the 80th minute.

Arsenal far from their best, but it is another one-goal win in the Premier League. They are now six points clear at the top of the Premier League, and it is 27 points from the first 30 available.

Another save at Craven Cottage, this time from Hornicek to keep out a sweet strike from Sessegnon at the edge of the box. Moments earlier, Dedic brought down Muniz at the edge of the box.

One last change by Arteta: Zubmimendi on for Guimaraes. Madueke could have made the points safe after slick interplay involving Eze and Merino around the edge of the box. Tried to be clever with the finish, the goalkeeper’s save was deflected back towards the goal and it was hacked off the line.

Leno gets down quickly to deny Lewis Hall as Newcastle pour forward in search of the winner.

As good as Murphy’s goal was, Rodrigo Muniz’s finish with the outside of his foot may well be better. Newcastle’s lead was short-lived.

And they have immediately conceded an equaliser, Rodrigo Munoz with a smart close range finish but Newcastle were be mortified with the defending.

Not for the first time Will Osula’s weak hold up play gives the ball back to Fulham but Newcastle had plenty of men back and failed to clear.

Jacob Murphy has scored the goal that some of Newcastle’s earlier good play deserved. Cut inside on to his weaker left foot, and curled a shot beyond Leno from 20 yards.

Newcastle finally have their goal and what a strike it is from Jacob Murphy. A loose pass from Cesar Palacios in midfield gifted possession to the Newcastle winger and he ran forward, cut inside on to his left foot and curled a shot beyond the reach of Leno from 25 yards.

Arsenal’s captain dinks over Tzolakis after Hull’s goalkeeper saved Madueke’s shot. Odeegard was on the move while the defenders were on their heels. Relief at the Emirates, this has been hard work for the champions. Can Hull muster a response?

This is about as dominant as Newcastle have been in a game all season but they have not been able to find a goal from some where. They have just gone close again as Will Osula just fails to reach Amar Dedic’s cross.

Arteta has made another double sub: Hincapie and Eze replace Tzolis and Calafiori, so all change down the left flank. For Hull, Lucas Herrington has replaced the injured Semi Ajayi at the back.

Felt like the cheers for Viktor Gyokeres to replace Kai Havertz were as much a reflection on frustrations with the German as enthusiasm for the Swede. Havertz has struggled today and is far less convincing in this sort of match – when the onus is more on the Arsenal to create – than he was in scoring the winner at Anfield last Sunday.

Specific explanation for Declan Rice’s substitution is unclear – he didn’t look injured – and so perhaps just reflection of risks associated with a third 90 minutes in six days alongside an acknowledgement of quality alternatives on the bench.

Some changes at the Emirates: Cho and Lewie Coyle are replaced by Joe Gelhardt and Brooke Norton-Cuffy.

Arteta with a double sub: Gyokeres and Merino on for Havertz and Rice, which comes as something of a surprise.

Hull’s goalkeeper Tzolakis has just saved with his feet to deny Madueke. A few minutes earlier, Konsa blocked a Cho shot after a threatening Hull break. Still no opening goal.

It has been all Newcastle at the start of the second half and Harvey Barnes has now failed to take a hat-trick of chances. This was the best of the lot, running on to the ball, middle of the area from a perfectly weighted Yoane Wissa pass, he blasted a shot the wrong side of the post.

The second halves at the Emirates and Craven Cottage have kicked off. No changes from Arsenal, Hull, Fulham or Newcastle.

While Kai Havertz was in an offside position when his shirt was pulled, he may not have been active.

The law states: “A player in an offside position is moving towards the ball with the intention of playing the ball and is fouled before playing or attempting to play the ball, or challenging an opponent for the ball, the foul is penalised as it has occurred before the offside offence.”

However, the Premier League match centre does not see it that way.

#ARSHUL – 42' VAR recommended an on-field review for a potential penalty to Arsenal for a challenge by Mendy on Havertz - but determined that Havertz was in an offside position in the build-up. REF: "After review, there is a holding offence by Blue No.32 on Red No.29. However, Red No.29 is in an offside position, so the final decision will be a free-kick for offside to Hull City".

Frustrating first half for Arsenal, but it was never going to be easy to go again in the Premier League following a Champions League away game and last Sunday’s big win against Liverpool. Hull have also played with confidence and resolve.

Hopefully there will be more action in the second 45 minutes at Craven Cottage and the Emirates.

An odd moment at the Emirates. Referee Farai Hallam was sent to monitor to look at a shirt pull on Kai Havertz in the Hull box. Through his headset, he tells the crowd that a “holding offence” has been committed – cue cheers from the Arsenal fans – before adding “however, the Arsenal player was in an offside position”. So no penalty. It would have been very soft in any case, Havertz was not close to reaching the rebound from a long shot.

Mikel Arteta predictably furious to not get a penalty, even though Havertz was clearly offside before being pulled.

I’ve spotted something else since I was covering Arsenal in 2018. I was gazing up at the boards that circle the top tier where the club mark every trophy that the team has won. There was previously this big gap, of course, from 2005 until 2014 before the cabinet gradually began refilling. But someone has since had a good idea. They have added all the women’s trophies as well, transforming the tally by just the odd 17 during those tricky years of transition and billionaire rivals owners in the men’s game.

Declan Rice has also just had a shot flash wide, but still no clear chances for either team.

Dan Burn thought he had scored from close-range, but Wissa has been penalised for blocking Castagne so the goal was ruled out.

#FULNEW – 33' The referee's call of no goal to Newcastle United was checked and confirmed by VAR – with it deemed that Wissa fouled Castagne in the build-up. AR2: "Defensive foul at the back post. Wissa holding at the back post". REF: "Castagne held back by Wissa. It's a deliberate foul". VAR: "He's clearly holding him - confirming the on-field decision of defensive free-kick. Wissa is clearly making an attempt to hold the Fulham defender. Clear holding offence".

Some controversy here as Newcastle appear to have had a perfectly good goal ruled out for an innocuous looking foul from Yoane Wissa on Timothy Costagne inside the box. Dan Burn was the scorer. Newcastle’s bench are furious. Most of us are just bemused.

Arsenal have largely been on top after Hull’s lively start but clear chances are scarce and Hull’s fans certainly sound increasingly confident. “You’re only here for the Tigers”, they say. To which a rendition of “champions” rings out. On that note, they had a replica of the Premier League trophy outside the Emirates before the match, with fans queuing up to get their photo with it.

The bright start from the visitors has not been maintained and Fulham have been on top for the last few minutes. Lukasz Hornicek just made a full-stretch save to deny Oscar Bobb and there is a sense inside the stadium that the first goal is coming.

Newcastle, as we have seen all too often recently, really struggle to pass through the lines and when the ball makes it out wide it invariably seems to go backwards again.

Although Harvey Barnes has just forced a good save from Leno from a Jacob Murphy cross. It was easily Newcastle’s best move of the match as Wissa and Osula linked up for the first time through the middle.

David Raya to Arsenal’s rescue, plucking Stroud’s shot away from the bottom corner after a Calafiori giveaway. This has been a positive opening 20 minutes from Hull.

Arsenal with a penalty shout, turned down.

Kai Havertz was going away from goal but Nobel Medy hardly subtle with his challenge – arm and leg – on the edge of the area. It would have been what we call soft but the sort of innocuous challenge that very often results in a foul elsewhere on the pitch.

On a self-indulgent note, this is an all-too-rare trip to the Emirates for me. I covered Arsenal for the last decade of the Arsene Wenger era, ending in 2018 and haven’t made many visits since. A few things are already striking: so many familiar faces still among the staff at a club that has always had more of a family feel than most. Outside, and few of the gaps in the middle (or top tier) that became evident during the final Wenger years when many fans clearly lost faith in the team’s title-winning credentials. The atmosphere is a notch up too.

Thiaw heads wide after a well-worked short corner from Newcastle, but the flag is up for offside in any case. Lewis Hall with the cross.

A decent start from Newcastle United against Fulham and you can see clearer patterns of play emerging. The fact that Fulham are constantly looking to build their attacks by playing out from the back has also helped. The home side have had a couple of scares already.

With the amount of games we’re playing, we need that freshness. The team has been performing really well, the finishers have been key again and that’s what we have today – start the game really, really strong and if we need to, have the ability to change the game.

The return of Sean Steur is a positive move from Newcastle manager Matthias Jaissle but is it also a sign the German is feeling the pressure of public opinion on Tyneside?

Mikel Arteta has said Arsenal are “looking after” Declan Rice more now than in any previous campaign and has rejected suggestions that the England midfielder is being overplayed.

Rice played through nerve pain in his hamstring in the second half of last season, when he made an extraordinary 69 appearances for club and country.

The 27-year-old has played at least 60 matches in each of the past four campaigns, prompting concerns among Arsenal supporters that he is at risk of burnout or injury.

Arteta kept Rice on the field for the full 90 minutes of Arsenal’s midweek victory over Slavia Prague but insisted the club is working harder than ever to manage the midfielder’s workload.

So far this season, Rice has been left out of Arsenal’s two Carabao Cup matches and also withdrew from the previous international break in September.

Asked if he was concerned about Rice’s fitness, Arteta said: “Concerns? Which is the only player, the only player in this squad, that for two games in the Carabao Cup has been left out of the squad? At home with two days off? It’s not true [that Rice is being overplayed].

“We are really looking after him more than any other season. The way he is training and all the love [he is being shown]. That’s it.

“Here are the demands of this football club and a player like him. Not only him, because there are many others that have even less time. That’s it. That has to be the mindset. If you start to put other things in your brain, [it will create] damage. Erase them, get them in the bin and just focus on what you have to do.”

Newly-promoted Hull City stunned Arsenal at the Emirates thanks to goals from Geovanni and Daniel Cousin in September 2008, but you will find long odds on them repeating the feat this afternoon.

With Manchester City playing at Nottingham Forest in the later kick-off, Mikel Arteta’s team have the chance to extend their lead at the top of the Premier League and make it 27 points from a possible 30 going into the international break.

Victory at Anfield last weekend was achieved with steel and resolve, but the more intricate side of Arsenal’s attacking play will be under the microscope against opponents likely to defend deep and in numbers.

With that in mind, it was noteworthy that Kai Havertz, Martin Odegaard, Bruno Guimaraes and Myles Lewis-Skelly started the Champions League win at Slavia Prague on the bench, with only Odegaard coming on.

Declan Rice played the full 90 minutes, and one wonders if this is a rare chance for Arteta to leave him out of a Premier League starting XI.

Hull are 12th and have lost only two league games, a performance which far exceeds the gloomy pre-season forecasts for their chances. However, they are winless since the end of August and have not kept a clean sheet since a goalless draw with Aston Villa on Sept 4.

There is one other three o’clock kick-off: Fulham host Newcastle United at Craven Cottage, with Matthias Jaissle under increasing pressure.

Newcastle were booed off at St James’ Park on Monday after their limp defeat by Everton, and have looked far too easy to play through all season, with their defensive statistics making for grim reading.

Alvaro Arbeloa was in Jaissle’s position a few weeks ago, but Fulham appear to have turned a corner and were impressive in a 1-1 at Villa Park last time out. There were reports of dressing room unrest, but Fulham rank highly for possession and chance creation so it appears the Spaniard is putting his stamp on things.

What we know about Krakow mass shooting so far

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

At least six people have been killed and several others injured after a gunman suspected to have ties to the far-right opened fire in central Krakow, the second largest city in Poland. Here's everything we know so far.

The attack began shortly before 15:00 local time (14:00 GMT) in Krakow's Main Market Square, the bustling central point of the city.

Police said a man drove into the square before beginning to fire "at random people from his car".

Footage from the scene shows scenes of panic as people fled, with the sound of gunfire clearly heard.

People run for shelter as gunshots heard in Krakow

Armed officers raced to the scene and engaged in a gunfight with the attacker, who was shot within three minutes of the attack beginning, preventing a further massacre, officials said.

The gunman was shot but is alive and has been arrested.

Distressing footage authenticated by BBC Verify shows the moment a police officer confronts the suspect on the ground before firing his weapon several times.

The officer then falls to the ground, apparently having been injured himself.

Moment police officer appears to shoot Krakow suspect

Shop windows in the square have signs of damage, seemingly from bullets, with glass shards scattered along the floor.

City officials have urged people who live in the city and tourists to stay away from the area while an investigation continues.

Six people - four women and two men - are confirmed to have been killed, including a foreign national.

Sixteen people, including the attacker, were taken to hospital. Among those being treated are two police officers who were shot while detaining the attacker.

One of the police officers was described as being in a "very critical condition" by a Polish government minister after being shot three times while arresting the attacker. The officer is undergoing surgery, he added.

Polish authorities have not confirmed the names and ages of the other victims.

Police have identified the attacker as a 25-year-old Polish man, but have not released any further details about his identity.

The suspect was wearing "military-style attire" and had a military-grade weapon, according to a police spokesperson. However, authorities have confirmed the attacker was not a soldier.

"Many pieces of weapons and ammunition were found in his vehicle", the spokesperson added.

Polish media has reported the suspect had a firearms permit.

A minister has said early investigation indicate he was inspired by "far-right ideologies", without elaborating further.

His home is being searched with support from counter-terrorism police.

The shooting happened in one of the largest medieval squares in Europe, a busy location which is also a popular tourist destination.

Jonathan Balshaw from Ipswich was there with his Polish girlfriend Dominika. The couple were outside St Mary's Basilica, in the corner of the square, when the shooting started.

Jonathan said: "We ran down the side of the church and through the side door and hid with several other people until the gunfire died down.

"A lot of shots were fired, more than 20 with it sounding like multiple different firearms - there were two distinctly different noises."

Caitlin Shasby from Newcastle arrived in Krakow yesterday with her friend Jon for a long weekend.

She said they heard shots from their hotel room but were not sure what was happening until their hotel rang them and told them to "stay in our rooms until further notice".

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk is on his way to Krakow and is expected to give an address later on Saturday.

Earlier, he said he was being kept informed by the interior minister and that police were investigating "the background and all the circumstances".

Krakow Mayor Åukasz GibaÅa has signed an order declaring a period of mourning in the city which will last until Wednesday, November 11.

President Karol Nawrocki said he was "deeply concerned" and was awaiting "a full explanation of the circumstances surrounding these tragic events".

US ambassador to Poland Tom Rose described it as a "savage and murderous attack".

The EU's top diplomat, Kaja Kallas, said the shooting was "a tragedy that will be felt far beyond Poland".
